# Admin Operations Guide

This guide covers day-to-day administration tasks for DumpFire. You need the **Admin** or **Superadmin** role to access the Admin panel, and part of it is the superadmin's alone.

## Accessing the Admin Panel

Click the **⚙️ Admin** link in the dashboard sidebar or navigate to `/admin`.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    Dashboard["Dashboard"] --> Admin["Admin Panel"]
    Admin --> Users["User Management"]
    Admin --> Teams["Team Management"]
    Admin --> People["People Directory\n(/admin/people)"]
    Admin --> Iso["ISO 27001:2022 Checklist\n(/admin/iso-27001, read-only)"]
    Admin --> Boards["Board Management"]
    Admin --> Danger["Danger Zone:\npurge, vacuum, XP"]
    Admin --> Settings["Application Settings\n(the App URL)"]
    Admin --> Backups["Scheduled Backups"]
    Admin --> SMTP["Email / SMTP"]
    Admin -->|"superadmin only"| Restore["Backup & Restore:\nexport and import"]
    Admin -->|"superadmin only"| Reset["Reset Everything"]
```

### The Admin Page's Dialogs and the Keyboard

Every action on this page that asks first (Clear Cards, Clear Categories, Delete Board, Delete User, Delete Team, Remove Destination, Purge Archived Cards, Vacuum Database, Reset All XP, Reset Everything), and the Archived page's Delete Permanently and Purge All, asks in the app's confirm dialog, the one the boards, All Tasks and the Teams page use (card 2362, 2026-10-21; each page had a dialog of its own before). As it opens, **Cancel** has the keyboard's focus, so Enter straight after cancels; **Tab** and **Shift+Tab** stay in the dialog; **Escape** cancels; and as it closes the focus goes back to the button that opened it. To confirm by keyboard, press Tab, then Enter. A click on the dark overlay around it does nothing. A dialog that asks for your password (card 2701: see [Your Password, Asked Again](#your-password-asked-again)) gives the focus to its **Your password** field instead, and asks for a code from your authenticator app under it (card 2770); Enter in the password field moves to the code field while that is empty, and Enter in the code field confirms once both are filled. It stays open, buttons greyed, until the answer comes: open still, with the server's words, for a wrong password, and closed for any other answer.

The **Import Database** dialog and Google Drive's **Connect** box keep their own look and behave the same way: Cancel has the focus, Tab stays in them (in the import, through the chosen way to restore, the password field, the code field and its switch, Cancel and Confirm), and Escape is Cancel. The invite link's dialog and the reset's notice have one button and no way out but it. As each opens its button has the focus (the invite link's **📋 Copy**), and Tab and Shift+Tab stay in it (card 2365, 2026-10-21; Tab walked the page behind them, and the reset's notice took no focus). The invite link's dialog does not close on Escape: the link is shown once, and only **Done** lets it go. After Done the focus goes to **+ Add User** when the link came from Create, or to the row's 📧 when it came from there (Create itself has gone with its form by then). The link of a two-step sign-in reset is shown in the same dialog, with its own words, and after Done the focus goes back to the row's 🛡️ (card 2773, see [Resetting Two-Step Sign-In](#resetting-two-step-sign-in)). The reset's notice closes on Escape when its button is **Close** (a reset that failed and changed nothing); after a reset that was done its one way out is **Continue to set-up**, and Escape does nothing.

The 🔑 password dialog (another account's **Reset Password**, or **Change Your Password** on your own row) holds the keyboard the same way (card 2886, 2026-11-17; before it the focus stayed on 🔑 behind it, Tab walked the page and Escape did nothing). As it opens the focus is in **Your password** (**Current password** on your own row); Tab and Shift+Tab stay in it; Escape is Cancel, clearing what was typed; and as it closes, by Cancel, Escape or a finished reset, the focus goes back to that row's 🔑. Enter in the password field moves to the code field while that is empty, and Enter in the code field, or in the password once a code is typed, moves on to the new password. Enter never sends a reset: a reset ends every session, API key and webhook of the account, so **Reset Password** (or **Change Password**) is pressed on purpose.

```mermaid
stateDiagram-v2
    [*] --> Open: a button that asks first
    Open: Dialog open, focus on Cancel
    Open --> Open: Tab / Shift+Tab, round its own buttons
    Open --> Closed: Cancel, or Escape
    Open --> Done: Tab to the confirm button, Enter
    Closed --> [*]: focus back on the button, nothing done
    Done --> [*]: the action runs
```

## Who May Do What

The superadmin is the account made when DumpFire was set up, and there is one. An administrator does everything on the page that is about people, boards, housekeeping, mail and the scheduled backups. The export, the import and the reset are the superadmin's:

| On the admin page | An administrator | The superadmin |
|-------------------|------------------|----------------|
| Database Overview | ✅ | ✅ |
| Board Management: delete a board, clear its cards or categories | ✅ | ✅ |
| User Management, within [whose account they may manage](#modifying-users) | ✅ | ✅ |
| Team Management | ✅ | ✅ |
| [People Directory](#people-directory) (`/admin/people`, card 2674) | ✅ | ✅ |
| [ISO 27001:2022 Checklist](#the-iso-270012022-checklist) (`/admin/iso-27001`, card 2670), read-only | ✅ | ✅ |
| Danger Zone: Purge Archived Cards, Vacuum Database, Reset All XP | ✅ | ✅ |
| The system log and the audit trail (`/audit`) | ✅ | ✅ |
| **Application Settings**: see the App URL | ✅ | ✅ |
| Application Settings: **set the App URL** | ❌ (card 2847; ✅ from card 2395 until then) | ✅ |
| **Scheduled Backups**: the schedule, the destinations, "Test", "Run Now" and Google Drive's "Connect" | ✅ (since card 2395) | ✅ |
| Scheduled Backups: **lower Keep Last**, **remove a destination** (a rename is a removal) or **switch the schedule off** | ❌ (card 2398) | ✅ |
| Scheduled Backups: **switch Email on failure off** or **change who receives it** (ticking it on stays every administrator's) | ❌ (card 2408) | ✅ |
| **Email / SMTP**: the mail settings, the test mail and what mail has done | ✅ (since card 2395) | ✅ |
| **Backup & Restore**: Export Backup and Import Backup | ❌ | ✅ |
| Danger Zone: **Reset Everything** | ❌ | ✅ |

The last two are the user list by another way, which is why an administrator, who cannot manage another administrator's account, cannot use them. An export holds every password hash. An import replaces every account with the backup's. Whoever sets up after a reset is the superadmin.

**The mail and backup settings are every administrator's since card 2395 (2026-10-21), at the owner's request; they were the superadmin's alone.** That gives every administrator a way round the rule that only the superadmin manages an administrator, and you should know what it is:

- **An administrator who sets a backup destination can receive a full copy of the database**: every account's password hash and email address, every board, card and comment. They can add a destination of their own and press Run Now.
- **An administrator who sets the mail server sees every mail DumpFire sends**, invite links and password-reset links included, the superadmin's among them; whoever reads such a link can set that account's password. The App URL, saved with the mail settings, decides where every link in a mail points, and **is the superadmin's alone since card 2847** (2026-11-17): an administrator could point it at a server of their own that looks like DumpFire and collect the password and code of whoever followed a link. The mail server stays every administrator's; whether it should be the superadmin's too is card 2906, for the owner to rule.
- **The stored secrets stay where they were saved.** Since card 2898 they are also kept sealed in the database, under a key derived from the MFA key file, each bound to where it was saved (the mail connection, the destination's place and host key, the webhook's address): a hand edit of the database that moves one makes it unusable, and it is then shown empty and has to be typed again. Since card 2928 every webhook's address is kept sealed the same way, and no backup or export holds one: a restore onto this server puts each back, and any other names each webhook whose address is to be sent again with `PUT /api/v1/webhooks/{id}`. The mail password and every destination's password, key and token are masked on the page for everybody, and a save or a Test that points a masked one at another host, bucket, folder or path is refused: it has to be typed again. Two exceptions: an administrator may confirm the host key of an SFTP destination that has a stored password but no confirmed key yet, and the stored password then goes to whichever server shows that key; and **Connect** on a Google Drive destination already set up with a client ID and secret ties it to the Google account of whoever connects, using the stored ID and secret (a `WARN` line under `BACKUP` in the system log where it replaces a connection the destination had).
- **Lowering Keep Last, removing a destination and switching the schedule off are the superadmin's** (card 2398, 2026-10-22). Lowering **Keep Last** (its least is 1) and pressing **Run Now** deleted every older copy at every destination, and removing the destinations or switching the schedule off stopped the copies, so an administrator could leave you nothing to restore from; it was logged, not prevented. Now the server refuses each of the three to anyone but the superadmin, `403` "Lowering Keep Last, removing a destination, switching the schedule or the failure mail off, or changing who receives the failure mail needs the superadmin." followed by what the save would have done, and nothing is saved. A destination renamed, or saved as another type, is the old one removed, and is refused too: add the new one and ask the superadmin to remove the old. An administrator can still raise Keep Last, change between schedules that run, add destinations and edit them in place, test, connect and run backups. On the page they see 🗑️ dimmed and the schedule's option reading **Disabled (superadmin only)**, each saying the rule when chosen, Keep Last putting a lower number back, and one line under the schedule; editing a destination, a line under its name says renaming needs the superadmin. For anyone, a save the server refuses reads the settings again, so the panel shows what is saved, and the message ends "The settings shown are the ones the server holds." **The way round it is closed too** (card 2405): pressing **Run Now** Keep Last times used to push every older copy out, as each press added a copy and Keep Last kept the newest. Now Keep Last counts Run Now copies and scheduled copies separately, so a Run Now can push out only older Run Now copies, never a scheduled one: see [Manual Backup](#manual-backup). A schedule made more frequent still keeps Keep Last copies over a shorter time. Each Run Now is in the backup history, and each schedule change a line under `BACKUP`.
- **The failure mail is the superadmin's to switch off or send elsewhere** (card 2408, 2026-10-22). The failure mail is how you hear that the copies have stopped, and any administrator could untick **Email on failure** or put their own address in its place, so that a backup left failing was heard of by nobody else. Now the server refuses both to anyone but the superadmin, with the same `403` and nothing saved: unticking it where it is ticked, and any change to its addresses, an address added beside yours included, the addresses cleared, and a change made while it is unticked (so they cannot be set first and the box ticked after). The same addresses written in another order, in capitals, or separated by semicolons are not a change. Ticking it on stays every administrator's: it only adds a mail. On the page an administrator sees **Email on failure** dimmed while it is ticked (unticked, it ticks itself again and says the rule) and its address box dimmed and read-only (typing or pasting in it says the rule); the superadmin's are as before. A save sends the failure mail's settings only where they were changed on the page, so a page left open does not put back, or get refused for, a change the superadmin made since. A backup an administrator makes fail (a destination edited with a wrong password or secret) is not quiet: the failure mail goes to the addresses the superadmin chose. Two quiet ways remain, each a `WARN` line in the system log under their name and not prevented: pointing a destination, in place, at a store of their own with its secret typed again (every run succeeds, so no failure mail goes, and the copies go to their store from then on), and changing or clearing the mail settings (above), which silences every mail, the failure mail included.
- **An administrator can probe the inside of your network.** The SMTP host, an SFTP host and an S3 endpoint may be any address, addresses inside the network included (the server itself, the Docker host, internal ranges, `169.254.169.254`), and what **Send Test** and **Test** answer tells whether a port answered: a simple internal port scan. A destination's **Test** reaches only an address already saved (card 2939), so the probe needs a save, which asks for the administrator's password and code. Webhooks refuse addresses inside the network; these settings do not, because real setups use a NAS or a mail relay on the local network.
- **The settings go with whoever pointed them where they are** (card 2397, 2026-10-22). DumpFire records, for each place it has been, who pointed mail there (the mail **Host**, **Port**, **Username**, and the box that allows mail without a checked, encrypted connection), who set the App URL to each address, and who added each backup destination, pointed it at each place or account, or connected its Google Drive; a setting belongs to whoever is recorded for where it is now. Correcting the sender's name or address, typing a password or secret again for the same server or place, clearing the App URL, or confirming an SFTP host key makes nobody the setter: an administrator who corrects one of your fields does not take over, and on their demotion take away, your settings. Nor does one who changes a setting and changes it back (a relay's **Port**, a destination's prefix, the App URL): back where you put it, it is yours again, unless they typed the password or secret with the move, which makes the mail or destination run on what they typed and so theirs. When the setter is made a user, or deleted, what it set is removed in the same step: the mail settings (so **no mail is sent**, invites and password resets included, until an administrator sets the mail up again), the App URL (links then use the server's `APP_URL`, or else its `ORIGIN`), and its destinations with their stored secrets (the copies already at a destination stay, and a backup already running finishes). The superadmin is the same: lowered to user, what they set goes. Before you confirm, the delete's confirmation, and for a role change to **User** a confirmation of its own, says what would go, as the server has it the moment you press (another administrator may have taken a setting over since the page loaded); where that cannot be read, it asks all the same and says that whatever of those settings is still theirs goes too; afterwards a ⚠️ message that stays until closed says what went, and the panels that showed it read their settings again; the system log has a `WARN` line under `SMTP` and one under `BACKUP` naming it. Settings saved before the update that brings migration 0059 have nobody recorded and stay in force whoever is demoted: the first change that moves one (the **Port**, say, or a destination's path) makes whoever saves it its setter, and changed back it is nobody's again. A move by the superadmin, or one made with the password or secret typed, is always the mover's, even to a place another administrator visited first. A change made from the host's command line runs none of this until the server next starts (card 2406): every start, before any scheduled backup, removes what a setter who is no longer an administrator set, by the same rule, with the same `WARN` lines saying `found at start-up`. So after making an administrator a user from the host, restart the container to take their settings away. **Import Backup** does the same once a restore is committed (card 2411): a setter who is not an administrator in the restored data (a backup taken while such a change waited for a restart, say) loses those settings at once, whichever way it is restored, with the same lines saying `found after a restore`, and not at the next start. An account deleted from the host with foreign keys on, which the documented `node -e` command has, takes its record of what it set with it (the database removes those rows with the account), and its settings then stay in force as settings nobody is recorded for; deleted with foreign keys off (the `sqlite3` shell's default), its record stays, and the next start removes its settings as it does a demoted setter's. Delete accounts on the admin page, not from the host. Each change to the backup settings, and since card 2396 each change to the mail settings and the App URL, is also a line in the system log under the account that made it (the mail user name and password by name alone, never their values).

An administrator does not see the export, the import or Reset Everything on the page. In place of Backup & Restore is one section, "The superadmin's", which says what they are and names the superadmin. The routes behind them refuse everybody else with `403` and "This belongs to the superadmin", so a request made by hand fares no better. A demotion takes effect at once: an administrator made a user while a save, a Run Now, a Test, a Connect or a Send Test is on its way is refused at the moment it would be saved or sent, with nothing saved and nothing sent.

If nobody can sign in as the superadmin, no page puts it right: see [If Nobody Can Sign In as the Superadmin](deployment.md#if-nobody-can-sign-in-as-the-superadmin).

## Your Password, Asked Again

**Forty-four actions ask for your own password and a code** (cards 2701, 2697 and 2679, 2026-11-04, card 2773, 2026-11-13, cards 2771, 2772 and 2751, 2026-11-17, and card 2671, 2026-11-18): card 2701's eighteen, card 2773's 🛡️ (resetting another account's two-step sign-in) and card 2671's two, ⛔ (disabling an account) and ✅ (enabling it again), in the table below, every time, card 2771's three on the Account page (changing your email, changing your password, which the 🔑 dialog on your own row uses too, and **Generate Key**), every time, card 2772's five on the Account page (**Add an authenticator**, **Remove** on an authenticator, **Make new recovery codes**, **That was me** on the warning at the top of the page, and **Keep this key** on an API key made before two-step sign-in), every time, card 2751's three on the **Reports** page (making a schedule, a change to one that adds an address, turns it on or widens its **Include** filter, and **Email** on a report), card 2697's eight on decisions and the [people directory](#people-directory) (a participant's account and address, a decision's delete and withdrawal once it was opened, and adding, changing the address or account of, marking active (since card 3020), and deleting a person, and Add from accounts), card 2922's keep or remove of a setting held for review, card 3020's **Release** under Mail held in the Requests inbox, and card 2679's two on a decision's results page, every time: **Rule…**, which rules for good and mails every participant the ruling and its reason, and **Release results…**, which shows the totals and comments, for good, to everyone who can see the decision. Each of them destroys data in bulk or for good, makes, removes or changes an account's access, or sends the database, the mail that invites and resets go through, or a board's activity, somewhere the person pressing it chooses. Being signed in, and having the right role, used to be enough for all of them, so whoever had your signed-in browser (an unlocked machine, a copied cookie) or ran script in a DumpFire page could do any of them as you. Now they also need your password and a code from your authenticator app (or one of your recovery codes) with it: the dialogs ask for both (card 2770), and the server checks both (card 2771, released together with 2770); the action runs only when both are right. There is no "recently confirmed" spell: clearing five boards asks five times.

| Where | Action | Asks |
|-------|--------|------|
| Danger Zone | Purge Archived Cards, Reset All XP, Reset Everything | Always |
| Board Management | Clear Cards, Clear Categories, Delete | Always |
| User Management | Add User (a **Your password** box and a code box in the form), Change of role, 🔑 on another account's row (a **Your password** box and a code box in the dialog), 📧, 🛡️ (card 2773), ⛔ and ✅ (card 2671), 🗑️ | Always |
| Backup & Restore | Export Backup, Import Backup | Always |
| Scheduled Backups | Add Destination, Save of an edited destination, 🗑️ Remove, ✅ Confirm this key, ▶ Run Now, 🔗 Connect (a **Your password** box and a code box in the Connect box) | Always. A save of the schedule, Keep Last or the failure mail alone asks nothing |
| Email / SMTP and Application Settings | Save SMTP, and Save of the App URL (it is saved with the mail settings; the superadmin's alone since card 2847) | Always |
| The dashboard, a board's page | Delete Board, Delete Sub-board (from a card's window or the board's ⋯ menu) | Always |
| A board's page | Delete Column | Only when the column holds a card, archived ones included |
| The Archived page | Purge All | Always |
| Reports (card 2751) | **Email** on a report (a **Your password** box and a code box in the dialog), **+ New Schedule** (in the form), switching a schedule **on** (the **Turn on this schedule?** dialog, naming every address it will email) | Always, to any address, your own and the company's among them. Switching a schedule off, deleting it and **Use my time zone** ask nothing; so does any change to a schedule through the API that adds no address, does not turn it on and does not widen its **Include** filter |

A change of another account's email address through `PUT /api/users/{id}` asks too; no page offers one. **Not asked**: Vacuum, a destination's **Test**, **Send Test**, deleting a team, and every single card, comment, label, milestone or category delete, an empty column's, and an archive or restore. Your own password, email and API keys on the Account page asked for the password already; since card 2771 they ask for a code beside it, through the same check as every other action here (each form has the same password field, code field and **Use a recovery code instead** switch), and a script that calls those routes sends `currentPassword` and `currentCode` (the email and key routes no longer read `password`). The API's `DELETE /api/v1/boards/{id}` and `DELETE /api/v1/columns/{id}` ask nothing: a key has no password, and keys gaining scopes is card 2703.

**How it asks.** The dialog the action already had gains a **Your password** field, with the keyboard's focus in it; your browser's password manager may fill it. Under it is a **Code from your authenticator app** field (card 2770; a numeric keypad on a phone, which may offer the code), and a link-style button, **Use a recovery code instead**, turns the same field into **Recovery code** for one of the codes you saved when you set up two-step sign-in (**Use a code from your authenticator app instead** turns it back; switching empties the field, and each opening of a dialog starts with the authenticator code). **Confirm** stays greyed until both fields are filled, a code of spaces counting as empty, and Enter in the password field moves to the code field while that is blank, and Enter in the code field confirms once both fields are filled, so Enter can never send with the code missing. The code is sent as you typed it, beside the password, as `currentCode`, and is emptied from the page when sent, when the dialog is cancelled and after any refusal. After a wrong answer the password is emptied too and the focus goes back to the password field, as the server never says which of the two was wrong. After the three refusals the server decides before it looks at the password (card 2771), the password you typed is kept and the focus goes to the code field, for a new code alone: "That code has just been used. Wait for the next code from your authenticator app and enter it. Nothing was changed." (the same code sent again before the app shows the next), "That code is not in the right form. Enter the 6 digits from your authenticator app, or choose 'Use a recovery code instead'. Nothing was changed." (not six digits, nor a recovery code: a recovery code pasted into the authenticator field is cut short at seven characters), and "Your authenticator cannot be checked on this server right now. Use one of your recovery codes, or ask an administrator. Nothing was changed." (the server's MFA key is missing or another; a recovery code still works). None of the three counts toward the three wrong answers below. **Add from accounts** and the Person dialog on the [people directory](#people-directory) ask the same way. While the answer is awaited both buttons are greyed and **Confirm** reads "Working…", so one press sends one request. A request that gets no answer at all is said in the dialog, "No answer from the server. Try again.", with the field ready to type in again. **Add User** and Google Drive's **Connect** ask in the same fields, and say a refusal of them beside the fields the same way. The 🔑 dialog (on your own row too) asks for the code in the same way, but Enter in its code field does nothing yet: card 2886 will move Enter there to the new-password box. A wrong password or a wrong code is said in the dialog itself, "Your password or code was not right. Nothing was changed.", both fields are emptied and the focus put back in the password, and nothing was changed; anything else the server refuses (the board was deleted in another tab, say) closes the dialog and is said as before. A column whose every card is archived shows no field at first, because the page cannot see those cards; the server's answer, "Enter your password and a code from your authenticator app to confirm this. Nothing was changed.", then shows the fields in the same dialog. A tab opened before the release that brings cards 2770 and 2771 sends no code and gets the same answer: reload it. The password and the code are sent once and cleared from the page: never in a toast, the address bar or the browser's storage, and never in the system log, which says only "Password and code confirmed by *name* (ID: *n*) for *action*", or "Password and a recovery code confirmed by …" beside a `WARN` line "A recovery code confirmed *action* for *name* (ID: *n*): *k* left". A recovery code used this way is spent, as at sign-in, and its owner is mailed "A recovery code was used to confirm a change on DumpFire" with the number left. A code is spent once it has confirmed an action, even when the action is then refused (your password changed meanwhile, say): wait for the next code to try again.

**Three wrong answers sign you out.** Each signed-in browser has a count of the attempts it has had checked without a right password and a right code together, kept with its session in the database, so a restart does not clear it, and with no time limit: only a right password with a right code sets it back to nought. A wrong password and a wrong code count alike, once for the attempt, or, since card 2772, once for each authenticator that can be checked on this server when the code is from an authenticator app: with two authenticators two wrong answers sign the browser out, with three one does, and the dialog says so under the code field before you send (a recovery code counts once). An authenticator whose secret this server cannot open is skipped, and not counted; the dialog's line counts every authenticator the account holds, so in that rare case it says more than is counted, never less. Only a password that is actually checked counts: one turned away unchecked, because the server is busy, the browser already has one being checked, or the code was refused before the password was looked at (above), never counts, so it can never sign you out. The wrong answer that takes the count to three or more, on any of the forty-two (the Account page's email form, password form and Generate Key, and the 🔑 dialog on your own row, among them), ends that browser's session. The dialog then says "Nothing was changed. This session has been signed out, after three wrong passwords or codes, or because it had ended: sign in again.", takes the password field away, and the page goes straight to the sign-in page. Sign in again: the new session starts at nought. Your other browsers stay signed in, and sign-in itself is not slowed or held up by it. The Account page's own words ("Password is incorrect", "Current password is incorrect") are gone: it says the same as every dialog. **A right password with a wrong code** is the clearest sign that someone else knows the password: it raises the account's security banner, as a wrong code at sign-in does, and, with mail set up, mails the owner "Someone entered your DumpFire password correctly on *time* from *address* while confirming a change, and then a wrong code." (at most once an hour); the system log has a `WARN` "Wrong code for user *n* on *action*: the password was right". It does not pause or lock the account's codes at sign-in. One browser checks one password at a time: a second sent while the first is being checked is answered "Your password is already being checked for this session. Wait for that answer and try again. Nothing was changed.", and is not counted, and never signs the browser out, even with three authenticators, when the first has already counted three while it is checked (card 2772's code review, CR1). If your password is changed or reset while an action that asked for it is being saved, the action is refused, "Your password was changed while this was being saved. Nothing was changed. Try again.", and the password you typed is not checked a second time. The system log has a `WARN` under `AUTH` for each wrong one, and "Session ended after three wrong current passwords for user *n* on *action*" for the end. So a stolen browser gets three guesses, not one an hour.

**The import confirms first.** An import's request is the file, so the password and the code cannot go in it, and should be proved before the server reads a byte of the file. **Import Backup** opens the file picker first. The file you choose is checked in the page (a `.db` backup that can be read) and then the **Import Database** dialog opens, naming it, with the two ways to restore, a **Your password** field and the authenticator code field under it (or a recovery code, by the switch). **Confirm**, once both are filled, sends the password and the code alone, and as soon as the server accepts it the dialog closes and the file is uploaded, carrying the server's one-use confirmation, good for one import from this browser for 60 seconds: the upload starts at once, so the 60 seconds never have to cover the picker. Nothing is uploaded before the password and the code are accepted. A wrong password or code keeps the dialog open and sends no file; **Cancel** lets the file go. A second import asks for the password again. An upload that reaches the server after the 60 seconds, or is sent twice, is refused with "Confirm the import with your password again: the confirmation was not found, was used or has expired. Nothing was changed.": press **Import Backup** again. So is an import whose account's password was changed, or that stopped being the superadmin, while the file was on its way.

**The export asks too.** **Export Backup** opens a dialog for your password and a code, and the file is saved only from the server's answer; a wrong one saves nothing. The file holds every account's email address and password hash. A request for it by hand is now a `POST` with `{ "currentPassword": "…", "currentCode": "…" }` (the code checked since card 2771): the old `GET /api/admin/export` is answered `405`.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    Press["You press an action that asks"] --> Field["The dialog asks for your password<br/>and a code (or a recovery code)"]
    Field --> Sent["Confirm, once both are filled: they are sent once, and cleared from the page"]
    Sent --> Refusable{"Refused for a reason that needs no password?<br/>(not yours to do, gone, a field wrong)"}
    Refusable -->|"yes"| Said["Said as before: nothing guessed, nothing counted"]
    Refusable -->|"no"| Form{"The code: in its form, not just used,<br/>and the authenticator checkable here?"}
    Form -->|"no"| Kept["'That code has just been used…', 'not in the right form…'<br/>or 'cannot be checked…': not counted,<br/>the password kept, a new code to type"]
    Kept --> Field
    Form -->|"yes"| Right{"Your password and code?"}
    Right -->|"right"| Done["Done, and the count goes back to nought"]
    Right -->|"wrong, first or second"| Again["'Your password or code was not right. Nothing was changed.'<br/>The dialog stays open, both fields emptied"]
    Again --> Field
    Right -->|"wrong, third"| Out["This browser is signed out.<br/>Sign in again"]
```

## When Something Is Not Done

This page, the Teams page, the Reports page's schedules, All Tasks, the Archived page, every change made on a board's page (since card 2280, 2026-10-20), a board's Archived Cards panel, a card's window and its **Link existing board**, the share window, a subtask's window, the dashboard and My Account each say when a change was not made, in the server's own words where it gave any: "No edit access to this board", "Card not found", "You can only delete your own templates". Several of them used to say a change had worked whatever the answer was, or to say nothing.

- **A message that something was not done stays** until it is closed with ✕ or the page is left. Most are in the corner of the page. A card's window says it at its top, where it stays in view however far the window is scrolled. An import says it in the report under the **Import Backup** button, and not in the corner as well. The mail settings say it under their buttons, and the Reports page's emailing of a report in its dialog, each until it is tried again. The share window and a subtask's window say it on a line of their own above their buttons, with a ✕ to close it; My Account says it under what was refused, the avatar, the mail switches or the API key panel; and the **New Sub-board** form of a board's menu on the dashboard on its own line. Each of these goes when that change is tried again.
- **The corner shows each such message once, and three at most.** The same message again takes the place of the one shown, and a fourth puts the oldest away. A message about a change made on a page you have since left is not shown on the next page.
- **A message that something was done goes after three seconds**, as does any note or warning. Not every change has one: most of what is done in a card's window (a label, the cover, an assignee), and turning a report schedule off or deleting it, show the change itself and say nothing more. Turning one on asks for your password and a code first (card 2751), in its own dialog.
- **What the page changed before the answer came is put back**: a role in a user's row, a backup destination added or removed, a card's cover, a card's milestone, a person's role in the share window, a board's star on the dashboard, and your avatar and mail switches on My Account. A card's window in All Tasks, All To Do or a plan page that could not save stays open with what was typed in it (a plan page's said "Card updated" and closed whatever came back, until card 2281, 2026-10-20), and so do a board's **New Task** window whose card was not made (it said "Created" until 2026-10-15), a subtask's window and the forms for a new team, a new user and a new schedule. A subtask whose delete was refused stays on the card. A subtask's **Save** and the avatar on My Account cannot be pressed again while a change is on its way, so a double click makes one subtask. Removing someone, or a team, from the share window who no longer has the board (removed in another tab) says "This board is not shared with that user" (or team), where it said nothing.
- **A column's rename that is refused is said**, in the server's words, and the column keeps its old title (card 2134). A Complete or Done column that holds any card, archived ones included, can be renamed only to Complete or Done, and a column that holds any card cannot be renamed Complete or Done: the cards in it would become open work, or finished work nobody finished. An empty column is renamed freely. Keep a Complete or Done column that holds cards called Complete or Done: moving its cards out so as to rename it would reopen every one of them. To rename a column that holds cards into Complete or Done, move the cards first, each finished one to a Complete column and the rest to another column. It used to show the new title until the page was reloaded.
- **A change on a board is shown at once, and taken back if it is refused** (card 2280, 2026-10-20): the card window's **Save** of a saved card (the window stays open with what was typed), a title edited on the board itself, pinning a card, a priority changed from its dot, a card's menu or the bulk bar, the card menu's Move to, the completion prompt (which stays open with what was typed), adding and deleting categories, which show once the server has taken them (a refused one keeps its name in the box), dragging cards and columns, the board's name and emoji, and a column's colour, WIP limit and Add Task switch. Each is said in the server's words and only that change goes back; "Moved to …", "Pinned", "Priority → …", "WIP limit set" and "Title updated" are said only once the change is made. A bulk priority or archive changes every selected card at once, and the cards the server refuses go back, stay selected, and the count and the first reason are said. A card dragged into On Hold is moved first and its note written only once it has moved, so a refused move writes no note; a note refused after the move is said, and the note window stays open with it. Until then these looked saved until the page was reloaded. Sharing a board, a subtask's window, the dashboard's star, grouping and new boards, and the account page's avatar, mail settings and key revoke said nothing when refused until 2026-10-15 (card 2148).
- **A card's window opened from All Tasks, All To Do or a plan page saves as a board's does** (card 2281, 2026-10-20). **Save** sends only what was changed in the window, so a change a colleague made to another field of the same card meanwhile is kept; before, it sent every field back and undid theirs. A save with nothing changed closes the window quietly and sends nothing. A save that is refused, or gets no answer, is said in the server's words (or as "No answer from the server. The card may not have been saved.") and the window stays open with what was typed; on All Tasks and All To Do the change shows on the card at once and only that save's change goes back. "Card updated" is said only once the server has saved it. On All Tasks and All To Do, the fields not touched in an open window follow the card as the page reads it again.
- **A request that got no answer** is not known to have failed, and the message says so: "No answer from the server. It is not known whether this was done." Reload the page to see what was saved.
- **If your session has ended**, the message is shown and the page then goes to the login page. No dialog box stops it on the way.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    Act["You change something:<br/>save, delete, restore, assign"] --> Sent["The page sends it"]
    Sent --> Answer{"The answer"}
    Answer -->|"done"| Done["The page shows the change.<br/>Where a message says so too,<br/>it goes after 3 seconds"]
    Answer -->|"refused"| Refused["The server's reason, in the corner<br/>or in the window or panel that asked,<br/>which stays until closed or the page is left.<br/>What the page changed is put back"]
    Answer -->|"none"| None["'No answer from the server':<br/>it may or may not have been done"]
    Answer -->|"401: the session has ended"| Login["The reason, then the login page"]
```

---

## User Management

### Creating Users

1. Go to **Admin → Users**
2. Enter username, email, and role, and your own password in **Your password** (card 2701: making an account asks for it)
3. Click **Create User**. A wrong password is said in the corner, the form stays as typed and **Your password** is emptied
4. An invite email is sent automatically (if SMTP is configured), and the page waits for it: usually a second or two. "User created — invite email sent!" means the mail server accepted it
5. The user clicks the link to set their password, and is taken straight on to set up two-step sign-in with an authenticator app (card 2769). Only finishing that signs them in

**An account with no authenticator needs an invite or a set-up link to sign in** (card 2769). **An account made with a password you chose gets its set-up link as it is made** (card 2851): instead of the toast, a dialog opens, **🔑 Their set-up link**, with the link and 📋 Copy. Give the person the link and the password yourself, by a way only they read: the link is shown once, here, is never emailed, and works for seven days. They open it, sign in with the password and set up their authenticator app. If it is lost, or you could not be shown it (the dialog's words say so in the corner instead), give a new one with 🛡️ on their row (see [Resetting Two-Step Sign-In](#resetting-two-step-sign-in)). With no App URL, `APP_URL` or `ORIGIN` set there is no address to make any link from, and Create User is refused whether or not you typed a password. **An invite is spent when the password is set**, before set-up is finished: someone who sets their password and then leaves the set-up page needs a new invite (📧) or a 🛡️ link.

**When an invite's mail is not sent** (card 2196). Create User with no password, and 📧 on a user's row, wait for the invite's mail. If the mail server refuses it or does not answer, the invite is still made, and a dialog opens instead of the toast. "Not sent" means "not confirmed sent": a mail server can take the whole message and the connection then drop before it says so, and then the person receives the mail as well as you being given its link. Either one works; the first used sets the password:

> **📧 The invite mail was not sent**
>
> User nf-invitee was made, but the invite mail could not be sent: the mail server could not be reached.
>
> Give this link to nf-invitee yourself, by a way only they read. Whoever opens it can set the password of nf-invitee's account, then sets up its two-step sign-in and is signed in as them. It works once, for seven days, and stops working if another invite is sent to this account. Do not open it yourself.
>
> `https://kanban.example.com/invite/…` **📋 Copy**
>
> It is not shown again. If it is lost, send a new invite with 📧. Why it failed is under Email / SMTP below.

For 📧 the first line is the server's message: "The invite was made, but its mail to ana@example.com could not be sent: … Give ana the link yourself. Any earlier invite to this account no longer works." The reason is one of seven, never the mail server's own words, which can name the mail host: mail is not set up on this server; the mail password has not been entered on this server, and an administrator enters it under 📧 Email / SMTP; the mail server's certificate could not be checked, or it does not offer STARTTLS; the mail server could not be reached; the mail server refused the sign-in; the mail server did not accept the message; the mail could not be sent. Every administrator reads the mail server's words under **📧 Email / SMTP** and in the system log (the panel was the superadmin's alone until card 2395, and the dialog told an administrator "The superadmin can see why under Email / SMTP."). The panel is read again as the invite is answered, so it already shows this mail.

**When no invite can be mailed at all.** With no mail host, or with a mail user name saved and no password (what an Import Backup leaves until the password is typed again: a backup does not hold it), 📧 and Create User with no password are refused before anything is made, and any earlier invite to the account still works: `The invite was not made: the mail password has not been entered on this server; an administrator enters it under 📧 Email / SMTP. Any earlier invite to this account still works.` Create User says `Password must be at least 8 characters (…)` with the same reason; give a password, or type the mail password under Email / SMTP and try again. While a link's dialog is open, every 📧 and Create User stay greyed until **Done**, and 📋 Copy has the focus.

- **The link is a password.** Whoever opens it sets the account's password, then sets up two-step sign-in with their own phone, and is signed in as that user (card 2769), as the dialog says. Send it by a way only they read (not a shared channel), and do not open it yourself: opening it shows the form, and setting a password with it ends every session and key of the account. It is shown once, only to you, and is cleared when you press **Done**; it is in no toast, no address and no history. DumpFire keeps only a one-way fingerprint of the link (its SHA-256, card 2849), never the link itself, so nobody can show it to you again, and reading the database does not give anyone a working link: if it is lost, send a new invite with 📧, which ends the old one. 📋 Copy puts it on the clipboard, which Windows' clipboard history keeps, and syncs to your other devices when that is on.
- **One invite at a time.** While an invite is out, every 📧 and Create User are greyed for the second or two it takes, and the pressed 📧 reads "…": a second press used to send a second invite, which ends the first one's link.
- **No link shown.** If, while the mail was out, you were signed out or made an ordinary user, the account became one you may no longer manage, or another invite was sent for it, no link is given: "The invite was made, but its mail could not be sent, and its link cannot be shown to you now. Send a new invite with 📧 once mail works."
- **The trail.** Each time, one `[WARN] [AUTH]` line on the Audit page's Live Console names the account and you, by id, and says whether the link was given: `Invite for user 12 was issued by user 3; its mail was not sent (the mail server could not be reached), and its link was given in the answer to their request`. It holds no link. The link works for seven days whether or not its holder stays an administrator, so when an administrator leaves, search the system log for "its link was given" lines naming them, and send each account named a new invite with 📧, which ends the old link.

### User Roles

| Role | Admin Panel | Manage Users | All Boards | Board Sharing |
|------|------------|-------------|------------|---------------|
| **Superadmin** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ Implicit | ✅ |
| **Admin** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ Implicit | ✅ |
| **User** | ❌ | ❌ | Only shared | Only if owner |

### Modifying Users

- **Change role** — choose **User** or **Admin** in the row's picker, then press **Change** beside it; choosing alone saves nothing (card 2376, 2026-10-21: the picker saved as it was chosen, so a keyboard user arrowing through it on Windows saved each role passed, Admin included). **Change** is dimmed, and does nothing, until a role other than the account's is chosen, and while the change is on its way, when a second press sends nothing and a screen reader is told "Saving *name*'s role…". A change refused or not answered is said, and the picker shows the account's role again. A role chosen and not saved is forgotten once the account is deleted, or the page reads it back gone or already holding that role. Only the superadmin can make a user an administrator, and nobody is made superadmin, so the picker and **Change** are on the superadmin's User Management alone; another administrator, who could give only User, sees the role as its badge with neither (the card 2376 review, 2026-10-21: every row had a one-choice picker and a Change that could do nothing). Any invite sent to the account earlier and not yet used stops working, because it was sent to the account as it was: the message then reads "Role updated. The invite sent to this account earlier no longer works: send a new one with 📧." **Changing a role also signs the person out** (card 2775): every session the account has ends, so they sign in again (the password and the authenticator code) and the new role's session limits apply from then on; an email change signs nobody out. **An administrator made a user loses the settings that are still where they set them** (card 2397): the mail settings, the App URL and the backup destinations they pointed where they are now are removed with the role change. **Change** first reads what the account holds at that moment, then asks, naming them: "Make ann a user? This removes what they set: …", and sends nothing until **Confirm**. Every change of role asks for your password in that dialog (card 2701), so an account that holds nothing, and a user made an administrator, are asked about too, naming nothing: "Make ann an administrator? You will be asked for your password and a code from your authenticator app." Where the read fails it asks all the same: "Make ann a user? What they set of the mail settings, the App URL and the backup destinations could not be read just now: whatever of it is still theirs goes too." The ⚠️ message, which stays until closed, says what went: "Role updated. What ann had set was removed: the mail settings, so no mail is sent, invites and password resets included, until an administrator sets them again in Email / SMTP; the App URL, so the links in mail point to the server's APP_URL, or else its ORIGIN; the backup destination "NAS"." The panels that showed it (Email / SMTP and Application Settings, or Scheduled Backups) then show what is left. See [Who May Do What](#who-may-do-what).
- **Reset password** (🔑) — type a new password for the user, and your own in **Your password** (card 2701); a wrong one of yours is said in the dialog, which stays open with the new password as typed. Every session they have ends, so they are signed out everywhere and sign in with the new one. Every API key they have ends too, and so does any invite they have not used yet; every webhook they set up is switched off, and every report schedule of theirs is paused (card 2168). The dialog says beforehand what there is, a line for each only above zero: "They have 2 API keys, which stop working", "They set up 3 webhooks, which are switched off." and "They have 1 report schedule, which is paused.", then "They are signed out everywhere, every API key they have stops working, any invite not yet used stops working, the webhooks they set up are switched off and their report schedules are paused." The message afterwards gives all four counts, zeros included: "Password reset: 1 session and 1 API key ended, 3 webhooks switched off and 1 report schedule paused". They are not mailed: you give them the new password, so tell them then. The password it replaces is recorded, by a fingerprint of its hash (card 2997): no import of an older backup brings it back
- **Send an invite** (📧) — asks first, for your password too (card 2701: an invite is a password reset by another way), then mails the user a new invite link, with which they set a password themselves. It works for seven days. Any earlier invite to that account stops working as soon as the new one is sent, and the message says so. The button reads "…" while the page waits for the mail; "Invite sent to …" means the mail server accepted it, and a mail not sent opens the dialog of [When an invite's mail is not sent](#creating-users), with the link to pass on yourself. Whoever opens an older link is told it no longer works and to ask an administrator for a new one. When the user sets a password with the link, every session and every API key they had ends, the webhooks they set up are switched off and their report schedules paused, as a reset does, and the invite page tells them so beforehand: "Setting a password here ends this account's 2 API keys, switches off the 1 webhook it set up, pauses its 1 report schedule and signs it out everywhere else.", each part only where there is one. **Not for an account that uses two-step sign-in** (card 2773): its 📧 is dimmed, its title reads "This account uses two-step sign-in. Reset it first, then send the invite", and pressing it sends nothing; the server refuses such an invite too, and an invite sent before the account set up its authenticator stops working once it has. The password the invite replaces is recorded, as 🔑's is (card 2997)
- **Reset two-step sign-in** (🛡️) — for someone who has lost their authenticator: see [Resetting Two-Step Sign-In](#resetting-two-step-sign-in)
- **Disable user** (⛔) — the account can no longer sign in, and what it held ends at once; **Enable user** (✅) gives back its sign-in; **Delete user** (🗑️) is offered once it is disabled, and permanently removes it. See [Disabling, Enabling and Deleting an Account](#disabling-enabling-and-deleting-an-account) (card 2671). 🔑, 📧 and 🛡️ are not offered on a disabled account

**An invite ends when the account changes.** An invite not yet used stops working when the account's email address or role is changed, by you on this page or by a direct call, or by the user on their Account page: the mail may be in a mailbox the account no longer has, or was sent before the account was made an administrator. Changing a username or an emoji, or sending the role the account already has, ends nothing, and no such change signs anyone out. Whoever opens the old link is told it no longer works, and why it might not. Send a new invite with 📧. Invites waiting at the update that brings migration 0054 were ended by it, whatever their account; send again with 📧 the ones still wanted. See [When an Account's Email or Role Changes](authentication-security.md#when-an-accounts-email-or-role-changes).

**Three wrong answers sign a browser out** (card 2701, 2026-11-04; it was ten in 15 minutes, which started again every 15 minutes and at every restart). The email form, the password form and Generate Key on the Account page each ask for the current password and a code (card 2771), and so do the 🔑 dialog on your own row and every action of [Your Password, Asked Again](#your-password-asked-again). They share one count for each signed-in browser, kept with its session, with no time limit, set back to nought only by a right password with a right code, and adding one only for a password that is actually checked, whichever part was wrong: the third wrong answer ends that browser's session, and it signs in again. A check turned away as busy, or because one of the browser's own is under way, is not counted. Another browser already signed in is not held up, and nor is signing in, so a user who is signed out this way can still sign in and change their password, and an administrator can still reset it. An account can have at most five current-password checks and sign-ins from remembered browsers going at once, plus the new hash of each password change it completes, which the password form makes after giving its place back; that cannot hold up sign-ins from other browsers, invites and set-up, which are served at least every fourth job the threads take. A sixth current-password check at the same moment is answered "The server is very busy checking passwords right now. Wait a minute and try again.", and a sixth sign-in from a remembered browser "A sign-in with this name is already under way from another browser. Wait a few seconds and try again, or sign in from a browser you have used here before." Nobody using the pages one at a time meets either. See [Guessing a Current Password](authentication-security.md#guessing-a-current-password).

**Five wrong passwords at sign-in.** After five wrong passwords at one account within 15 minutes of each other, from any number of addresses, every sign-in at that account from a browser not remembered for it waits 1, 2, then 4 seconds before it is answered, the right password included, with the Sign In button reading "Signing in…" meanwhile (card 2177); the right password signs in after the wait and clears it, and otherwise it lasts until 15 minutes pass with no wrong password. While it lasts, such browsers are also let in **one at a time** (card 2194): a second sign-in at the account at the same moment is refused at once, "A sign-in with this name is already under way from another browser. Wait a few seconds and try again, or sign in from a browser you have used here before." A browser that has signed in to the account before, or signed out of it, is remembered for a year, is never made to wait, and is refused only while five of the account's own checks are going at once. Every attempt from an address counts toward its ten in 15 minutes, and the ones refused (the one-at-a-time refusal, "Sign-in is very busy right now…") and the ones at a mistyped username stay counted when the user then signs in: only that user's own attempts at the account are given back. So an office behind one address, or on one IPv6 network, that retries a burst can find every sign-in from it refused for up to 15 minutes. A user who says sign-in has become slow, or keeps saying a sign-in is already under way, may be the target of someone guessing: they can sign in on a browser they have used before, or wait 15 minutes after the guessing stops. The waits beginning is one `WARN` line under `AUTH` on the Audit page's Live Console, `Sign-ins at "pat" (user 12) are being slowed: 5 wrong passwords within 15 minutes of each other`, and the first refusal `Sign-ins at "pat" (user 12) from browsers not known here are being let in one at a time`, each with the account as the line's user, beside the "Failed login attempt" lines, which name the account and the address and nothing that was typed (see [Logs](#logs)). See [Guessing a Password at Sign-in](authentication-security.md#guessing-a-password-at-sign-in).

**During an attack on an account, send an invite rather than set a password.** Setting a password (🔑) ends every browser remembered for the account, so its owner, on any browser, then meets the one-at-a-time refusal for as long as the attack lasts. An invite (📧) leads the browser that uses it to setting up two-step sign-in without the sign-in page, and the set-up, once confirmed, signs it in and remembers it (card 2769). An invite is refused for an account that already has an authenticator. It needs mail to be set up, and an invite is never sent for the superadmin's account, and for an administrator's only by the superadmin. Where none of that will do, the host's operator can turn the rule off for one sign-in: see [If an Attack Keeps an Owner Out](deployment.md#if-an-attack-keeps-an-owner-out).

**A reset or an invite costs the account its API keys.** Both are how you take an account back from whoever may hold its password or a session, so both end every key, and anything that uses one (a script, an integration, a scheduled job) is answered `401` until it is given a new key. That holds when a user has simply forgotten their password: the server cannot tell a recovery from a takeover. Before resetting the password of an account that a script or an integration runs on, look at the dialog's count. The dialog reads it again when it opens, and gives one only on the rows whose password you may reset. A user who changes their own password on the Account page keeps their keys, and the webhooks they set up, unless they tick the box ("End my API keys too, and switch off the webhooks I set up", or "Switch off the 1 webhook I set up" when they have no key left). Their report schedules are paused either way since card 2894 (2026-11-18): the Account page says so above the button, and afterwards how many, and they turn back on the ones they recognise on the **Reports** page, with their password and a code. See [What Setting a Password Ends](authentication-security.md#what-setting-a-password-ends).

**What a reset switches off, and getting it back.** A reset, or an invite used, also stops what someone holding the account could have left sending things out (card 2168). Every webhook the account set up that is on is switched off: the ones whose address or secret it last set, or that it last switched on, through `POST /api/v1/boards/{id}/webhooks` or a `PUT` that changed either or switched it on. Since card 2923 every one it set up that was already switched off (its owner paused it with a `PUT`, say) is held the same way, so that it too is given a new secret when switched on; and **the boards' owners are mailed**, once the change is done, one mail each listing the webhooks switched off and, apart, those held, with the kind of action that caused it and no account ("the account that set it up had its password reset by an administrator"); the system log names both accounts. The dialogs say so before you confirm: "Their boards' owners are mailed about them, and about any already switched off, which can now be switched on only with a new secret." A disable or a delete is told the same way, the account itself left out of the mail; a reset on the server's host mails nobody, and its command tells you to tell the owners. **Since card 2935, for a board's owner or an administrator, every webhook whose signing secret it could read goes too**, whoever set it up: every webhook on the server that has a secret, for an administrator; every one on the boards it owns, for anyone else. The password dialog shows how many; the two-step reset and disable confirmations say it in words. Each is switched on again with one `PUT`, which gives it a new secret for its receiver. An account that has provably read no secret (never signed in, no API key, no invite used, made after two-step sign-in was released) is left out. Making an administrator a user, or an owner of a board an editor or a viewer, or taking an owner off a board, holds the webhooks it could read and no longer can, in the same way; the role-change confirmation and the Share window say so. **Enabling a disabled administrator and then resetting it holds every webhook on the server that has a secret again**: the Enable confirmation says so for an administrator. Anyone who reset or enabled again an owner or administrator that may have been taken before this release runs `node scripts/mfa-reset.mjs --hold-readable <username>` on the host (see the deployment guide). Every report schedule of the account that is on is paused. Neither is deleted, because the account's own integrations and reports would go with an intruder's. Once you know which are genuine:

- **A webhook** is switched on again by the board's owner or an administrator, with their API key: `PUT /api/v1/webhooks/{id}` and `{"active": true}`. An editor of the board who sends that is refused `403` "Only the board's owner or an administrator can switch a webhook on. Ask the board's owner.", even for a webhook the editor set up and their own reset switched off: the owner switches it on, or the editor deletes it and creates it again. Whoever switches one on becomes its setter, so their own reset switches it off again. No page shows webhooks: `GET /api/v1/boards/{id}/webhooks` lists a board's, with each one's address for its owner or an administrator, and `DELETE /api/v1/webhooks/{id}` removes one nobody recognises. The reset's line in the system log names the webhooks it switched off by id (`switched off 3 webhook(s) they set up (ids 4, 9, 12)`). Since card 2752's code review (CS4) each is recorded as switched off for good, `setter access ended`, as one is when its account is deleted: switching it on gives it a new secret, in that answer, to give its receiver; an editor may not delete it; and nobody but the board's owner or an administrator may make another webhook to its receiver on that board.
- **A report schedule** is switched on again by its owner, on the **Reports** page, where it shows ⏸️ and only its owner sees it. Ask the user to look them over once they are signed in again. One switched on is sent at its next time, never at once for a run it missed while it was off (card 2159).

A webhook or a report schedule that a request was still creating or changing when the reset went through is not written: the request is answered `401`, since the key or the session it came in with has gone. See [What Setting a Password Ends](authentication-security.md#what-setting-a-password-ends).

**What a reset does not end.** A webhook nobody is recorded as having set up is left on by a reset: one registered before the update that brings migration 0056, and one whose setter's account was deleted. So is one somebody else set up or changed since. **Since card 2752, a webhook with no setter is switched off for good all the same**, at the first start after the update (it was held at delivery since card 2325), until the board's owner or an administrator switches it on, as below. Before that deploy, Query B in the release notes lists every one that is on, and each is taken over or deleted ([Deployment](deployment.md#webhooks-stop-when-their-setter-cannot-open-the-board-and-the-activity-report-reads-only-boards-you-can-open)); after restoring a backup older than 2026-10-18/19, run Query B again.

**A webhook switched off when its setter loses the board** (cards 2325 and 2752). A webhook is sent only while the account that set it up can open the board. Since card 2752, one whose setter loses the board is **switched off for good**, at the change that takes the access away: the setter taken off the board, its team's share removed, the setter taken out of the team, the team deleted, the board made private, or an administrator made an ordinary user. It is also switched off at every start, after every restore, and at its next event where none of those saw it (a row changed from the host). One nobody is recorded as having set up is switched off the same way. Giving the account the board back changes nothing: it stays off until the board's owner or an administrator switches it on. Until card 2752 such a webhook was only held, and sent again by itself once the account could open the board.

**What you see.** The system log has one `WARN` under `Webhooks` for each change, written once. For one webhook: `Webhook <id> on board <board> switched off: the account that set it up (user <id>) can no longer open the board (<cause>). It stays off until an owner of the board or an administrator switches it on.` (or `… switched off: nobody is recorded as having set it up (<cause>) …`), never the address. For more than one, one line names them all, the first 20 and then how many more (card 2899's code review, F1: a restore of a backup with many webhooks wrote a line for each): `22 webhooks switched off (<cause>): webhook 4 on board 3: the account that set it up (user 7) can no longer open the board; webhook 5 on board 3: …; and 2 more. Each stays off until an owner of its board or an administrator switches it on.` The cause names the change and who made it: "Sam was taken off the board by Ann", "the team Design's share was removed by Ann", "Sam was taken out of the team Design by Lee", "the team Design was deleted by Lee", "the board was made private by Ann", "Sam's role was changed to user by Ann", "found when the server started", "found after a restore by Ann" or "found when an event was sent". **The board's owners are mailed** at the same time: the board's creator and every owner on its member list, or, where it has neither, every administrator and the superadmin, one mail each per change, naming the board, each webhook's id and host, why and the cause, whatever their mail settings. A start or a restore mails only where the schedulers run: elsewhere one more `WARN` says the owners were not mailed, and why. Since card 2923 a webhook already switched off that a change holds has a line of its own: `Webhook <id> on board <board>, already switched off, is now held: the account that set it up (user <id>) had its access ended (<cause>). Switched on again by an owner of the board or an administrator, it is given a new secret.`, and the owners' mail lists it apart, under the subject "A notice about webhooks on your boards". The first start of that release holds, once, every webhook already switched off with no hold, whoever set it, and says which in one `WARN`; every start after holds those whose setter is disabled or not recorded, and says so only when it held one. A restore of a backup from before the release holds every one too, and a working copy holds them the same way. Nobody is mailed for those found at start.

**A team manager can switch a board's webhooks off** (card 2752, S6). Taking a member out of a team, or deleting the team, switches off the webhooks that member set on every board they can no longer open, though the team manager may not own those boards. That is the lever a team manager already holds over the member's access; the boards' owners are told who pulled it, and switch the webhook back on if it is wanted.

**Someone else's webhook on your board** (card 2752, S3). Whenever an account that is neither an owner of the board nor an administrator makes a webhook on it, the board's owners are mailed who made it, its id and its host. And while a webhook is switched off this way, nobody but the board's owner or an administrator may make another to the same receiver on that board, the same scheme, host and port, whatever the path, query or case (the code review's CS2): the create is refused `409` "A webhook to this receiver (the same scheme, host and port) on this board was switched off. Only the board's owner or an administrator can switch it on again, or add another webhook to it.", asked after every other check, so a malformed request is told its own fault first (CS3). Nor may anyone else delete it (CS1): an editor's delete is refused `403` "Only the board's owner or an administrator can delete a webhook that was switched off this way. Ask the board's owner." What remains (accepted): a colleague can still make a webhook to a different receiver, and the owners are told when they do.

**The Share Board window** counts them for the board's owner and an administrator (card 2329). ✕ on a person or a team whose removal would switch webhooks off asks first ("Removing Sam switches off 1 webhook they set up on this board. It stays off, even if they are given the board back, until you or an administrator switches it on again with an API key.", **Remove** or **Cancel**); the switch from public to private asks the same way (card 2385); and above the list it says "1 webhook on this board was switched off and sends nothing. Read why with your API key (GET /api/v1/boards/{boardId}/webhooks), and delete it or switch it on.", naming no reason, since an ended key (card 2893) switches one off too.

The board's owner or an administrator reads why with their API key, in `pausedBecause` from `GET /api/v1/boards/{id}/webhooks` or `GET /api/v1/webhooks/{id}`, word for word:

- switched off because the account that set it up lost the board: "This was switched off because the account that set it up could no longer open this board. It stays off if that account is given the board back. The address was chosen by that account: delete the webhook unless you recognise it; to keep it, switch it on with your own key, which makes you its setter and gives it a new secret, given in that answer and readable by the board's owner or an administrator, and update the receiver."
- switched off because nobody was recorded as its setter: "This was switched off because nobody is recorded as having set it up. Delete it unless you recognise the address; to keep it, switch it on with your own key, which makes you its setter and gives it a new secret, given in that answer and readable by the board's owner or an administrator, and update the receiver." Since card 2923's code review it too is given a new secret when switched on, and a secret sent with that switch-on is refused.
- switched off by a password reset, a reset of the two-step sign-in (card 2915), an invite used, a holder's own change that ends their keys, or the account disabled (card 2671) or deleted (the code review's CS4: these record `'setter access ended'`, and switching one on gives a new secret too), or held by one of those when already switched off, or found switched off with its setter disabled or not recorded (card 2923), or held because an owner or administrator who could read its secret had its access ended or its role lowered (card 2935), or because an API key that was given its secret was revoked, expired or passed its deadline (card 2947, below): "This was switched off because the account that set it up, or an account that could read its secret as the board's owner or an administrator, had its password or its two-step sign-in reset, ended its API keys when changing its own password, used an invite, was disabled, or was deleted, or, for one that could read it, lost that role; or because an API key that was given its secret was revoked, expired or passed its deadline; one already switched off then, or found switched off with its setter disabled or not recorded, is held the same way. Treat its old secret as exposed: change it at the receiver now, whether or not the webhook is switched on again, since until then the receiver trusts anything signed with it. Delete the webhook unless you recognise its address; to keep it, switch it on with your own key, which makes you its setter and gives it a new secret, given in that answer and readable by the board's owner or an administrator, and update the receiver."
- switched off because the API key that set it up ended (card 2893, below): "This was switched off because the API key that set it up ended: it expired, passed its deadline, was deleted or is not the setter's; or, for a webhook set up before keys were recorded, the account that set it up has no working key from then. To keep it, switch it on with your own key, which makes you its setter and gives it a new secret, given in that answer and readable by the board's owner or an administrator, and update the receiver."
- `null` for one that is sent, and for one switched off by a `PUT`; one still on and held by a change none of the writes saw begins "Nothing is sent: " until its next event switches it off.

**Delete it unless you recognise the address** (`DELETE /api/v1/webhooks/{id}`): the address was chosen by the account that lost the board, or by nobody known. **To keep it, switch it on**: `PUT /api/v1/webhooks/{id}` with `{"active": true}` alone. That makes you its setter, clears why it was switched off, and, for one whose setter lost the board or had its access ended, or whose key ended, gives it a **new secret**, 64 hexadecimal characters, given in that answer and readable by the board's owner or an administrator: update the receiver with it. The former setter knows the old secret and could forge deliveries to the receiver, which may be the company's own system. Sending a `secret` with that switch-on is refused `400`, with nothing written. One switched off because nobody was recorded as its setter keeps its secret: send `{"active": true, "secret": "<new>"}` to change it too. An editor's switch-on is refused `403`, as before. Whoever switches it on answers for it: a reset of their account, their losing the board, or the end of the key they switched it on with, switches it off again. Prefer the board's owner where they have a key.

**A webhook switched off when its API key ends** (card 2893). Each webhook records the API key that created it, last set its address or secret, or switched it on. When that key ends the webhook is switched off for good, `'key ended'`, as one whose setter lost the board is: revoked on the Account page (in the same change), expired, past its deadline (card 2772's for keys made before two-step sign-in), or deleted any other way, found at the next start, restore or event; and one whose recorded key is another account's. One set with no key at all (through a session) is switched off at its next event. The log line names the key by its id, never its name, prefix or hash: `Webhook <id> on board <board> switched off: the API key that set it up (key <id>, user <id>) has ended (<cause>). …`, with "no key recorded" where the key was deleted; a revoke's cause reads "API key #12 was revoked by Sam". The board's owners are mailed as above, and the account that set it up gets a mail of its own, "A webhook you set was switched off", telling it to ask the board's owner, while it can still open the board.

- **The revoke counts them first.** On the Account page each key's row shows how many webhooks revoking it switches off ("Revoking it switches off 2 webhooks": those it set up, and, for the account's last working key from before the release, the account's webhooks from then); ✕ (Revoke) asks "Revoke? It switches off 2 webhooks.", and afterwards says "Key revoked. 2 webhooks were switched off." (the code review's CS4).

**A webhook held when an API key that was given its secret ends** (card 2947). Every answer that gives a webhook's secret records the API key it went to. Revoking that key holds, in the same change, every webhook with a secret it was given since the secret last changed, whoever set it; a key that expires or passes its deadline does within five minutes (a sweep at every start and every five minutes), and ending an account's access with its keys holds what all its keys were given. Each is held as `'setter access ended'`: switching it on gives it a new secret. The boards' owners are mailed, naming no account, and told to treat the old secret as exposed: change it at the receiver now, since the receiver trusts it until then. The WARN names the key and its user by id.

- **The Account page says it first.** A key's row adds "and holds 3 whose secret it was given" beside what it switches off; a key with an expiry or a deadline says "When it ends, the 3 webhooks whose secret it was given are switched off, and each needs a new secret at its receiver."; ✕ asks "Revoke? It switches off 0 webhooks and holds 3 whose secret it was given.", and afterwards says how many were held.
- **The cost of rotating a key.** A script that lists webhooks with its key is given their secrets, so revoking that key, or its expiry, gives each of those webhooks a new secret, which its receiver needs. Prefer a key with no expiry for such a script, and rotate deliberately.
- **The first start of this release** counts every key used before it as given the secret of each webhook its account can read or set that was made before the key's last use, and holds at once what keys already expired or past their deadline may have read (see the release notes in [Deployment](deployment.md)).
- **Rotating a key** an integration's webhooks were set with.
