The Audit page has an `[ERROR] [BACKUP] Backup History row for <destination> could not be written, though <file> was uploaded to it` line, Backup History shows nothing for it, no failure mail is sent, Keep Last still removes older copies at that destination, and a scheduled backup is not tried again. After a **Run Now** the toast is a warning that stays: "Backup completed to 1 destination. NAS: Uploaded, but Backup History could not record it: database or disk is full. …". It was reported as failed, mailed, and tried again, which put a second copy at the destination. The usual cause is the disk beside the database being full: free the space.

**Copies are named to the millisecond** (card 2415): `dumpfire-backup-2026-10-22T02-00-30-123.db`, and `…-123-manual.db` for a Run Now. Two Run Now presses in one second made one name, so the second copy replaced the first and Backup History showed one run.

### Backup History

The last 30 backup runs are shown, a row for each destination of each run (card 2413), with:
- Destination name and type
- File size
- Duration
- Status (success or failed with error message)

The panel shows the newest five rows and **▼ Show all N entries**. Each backup adds one row a destination, and the rows of runs older than the newest 30 are removed then, all of a run's rows together. Until card 2413 the history kept the newest 10 rows, so with four destinations it showed under three runs. Opening the page only reads them (card 2412): it removed them each time it was opened.

**After a restart**, an hourly, 6-hourly or 12-hourly schedule counts from the last successful scheduled backup, not from a **Run Now** (card 2412): a Run Now pressed just before a restart used to move the next scheduled backup later once. A Run Now is told by its copy's name, `…-manual.db`; one made before card 2405 has no `-manual` and still counts. The history keeps the last successful scheduled backup's row however many Run Now presses or failures come after it (card 2413), though Backup History no longer shows it once it is older than the last 30 runs; until then ten such rows pushed it out, and the first check after the restart ran one at once. The row kept is the last one recorded no later than the server's clock (card 2416): where a clock that ran fast had recorded a later one, that one was kept instead, and after 30 runs the real one went.

**A daily or weekly backup is made once in its minute** (card 2414; replaced by card 2415 below, which keeps every case here). Where the server's clock is put back across a restart into the minute of the backup's time, or a second instance of the server checks in that minute, a backup that has already finished in that minute is not made again, and the Audit page has a `[WARN] [BACKUP] Scheduled daily backup at 02:00 not made: …` line saying so. One still uploading when the other instance checks is not seen, and a second copy is made. A **Run Now** pressed in that minute does not stop the scheduled backup. Only a backup recorded within the hour from the start of that minute counts: one stamped later by a clock that ran fast and was then put back does not stop the backups of the days after it, and each daily or weekly check while it is newest writes a `[WARN] [BACKUP] The last scheduled backup is recorded at …, later than the server's clock …` line (the review of card 2414).

**A daily or weekly backup missed is made once** (card 2415). Where the server was down, or busy, through the backup's time, the first check after it starts (a minute later) makes that day's or week's backup, and the Audit page has a `[WARN] [BACKUP] Scheduled daily backup at 02:00 was not made at its time, … made now.` line. A server started with a daily or weekly schedule and no scheduled backup since its latest time makes one within a minute. Setting the schedule, its time or its day while the server runs does not: the first backup is at the next time, even where that is a minute away (the time set to 02:00 at 01:59:40 runs at 02:00). The time is the server's own clock and zone (UTC in the container unless `TZ` is set); where it follows UK time, a backup at a time in the hour the clocks go forward is made at the same time an hour on (01:30 is made at 02:30 BST), and one in the hour that comes twice when they go back is made once, at the first.

**A backup that failed at every destination is tried again** (card 2415) 15 minutes later, up to three times, for every schedule; it waited for the next scheduled time, up to a week. Each try has its own failure mail, so an outage can send four in 45 minutes; after the third try, the backups wait for their schedule again until one succeeds. The Audit page says `Scheduled backup failed at every destination: tried again at …` for each. A backup that succeeded at any destination is not tried again. Nor is one where every destination failed before anything was sent (its credentials not entered, or an SFTP host key not confirmed): enter them in **Edit**, as the row says.

**What a restart does to a failing backup.** A restart makes the backup that is due once, a minute after the start. Where that backup had already failed before the restart, that one try is all until a backup succeeds: the server does not try it again 15 minutes later, so a server restarted again and again sends one failure mail a start and makes one SFTP login a start (a refused login tried four times a start could lock the account), and the Audit page says `This scheduled backup already failed before this start: this is its one try until a run succeeds, …`. Where it had not failed before, a failure is tried again three times as above.

**An hourly, 6-hourly or 12-hourly schedule after a restart** leaves out a backup recorded later than the server's clock (card 2415), and writes the same `… later than the server's clock …` line: such a row held every backup off until the real time passed it and one interval more.

A destination whose credentials are not entered is not tried, and its row says "This destination's credentials are not entered: a backup does not hold them, so they are entered again after a restore. Enter them in Admin, Scheduled Backups." (for a Google Drive destination connected with OAuth: "… enter its client secret and connect it again."), with the failure mail any failure sends (card 2169). It is what a destination shows after a backup is restored onto another server, until its credentials are typed in again with **Edit**. An SFTP destination with neither a password nor a key is tried as before, since some servers accept it, once its host key is confirmed. After a restore onto another server, then, such a destination, its key confirmed again, is tried against the real host at every scheduled run, and its failed logins may lock the account out there: enter its credentials, or remove it.

An SFTP destination whose host key is not confirmed is not tried either, and its row says "SFTP host key not confirmed: nothing was sent to this destination. In Admin, Scheduled Backups, press Test on it and confirm the key it shows." (card 2250; see [Configuring Backups](#configuring-backups)). A key that no longer matches, whose type the server no longer offers, or that the server cannot prove it holds, fails the run with the Test's words: `Backup failed: NAS → SFTP host key refused: the server at nas.example:22 …`.

### Restoring from Backup

A backup `.db` file is a whole database: its data, its structure and its own record of applied migrations. There are two ways to put one back, and they differ in how much of it they use.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    Download["Download the backup .db file"] --> How{"Is the server running\nand can you sign in?"}
    How -->|"Yes"| Import["Admin panel:\nImport Backup"]
    Import --> Choice{"As it is, or\na working copy?"}
    Choice -->|"As it is"| Rows["Rows replaced.\nStructure and migration record kept.\nThis server's secrets kept for the same accounts,\nif the backup is this server's own, unchanged"]
    Choice -->|"Working copy"| Quiet["Rows replaced, then report schedules and webhooks\nswitched off, backup destinations and mail removed"]
    Quiet --> Verify
    How -->|"No"| Stop["Stop container"]
    Stop --> Replace["Replace dumpfire.db,\nthen delete dumpfire.db-wal and dumpfire.db-shm"]
    Replace --> Start["Start container:\nmigrations run"]
    Rows --> Verify["Verify data"]
    Start --> Verify
    Verify --> Enter["Enter any secret the result\nor the start-up line lists"]
```

**In the application** (Admin → Backup & Restore → **Import Backup**, as the superadmin). No restart is needed. Choose the file first; the dialog then names it and asks for your password and a code, and the file is uploaded only once the password is accepted, at once (card 2701): see [The import confirms first](#your-password-asked-again).

On a server that still runs, restore this way, not by hand: it keeps this server's secrets, which a file put in place by hand cannot. Nine things are true of every import, whichever way it is restored:

- **The system log is kept as it was, and none of the backup's log lines come in** (card 2899). The system log is this server's record of what was done to it, and by whom, so an import no longer replaces it: every line written before the import, those written since the backup was made included, is still there afterwards, word for word, and an edited backup cannot put its own lines in their place. The backup's log stays in the file, where it can be read with `sqlite3 <file> "SELECT * FROM system_logs"`. Once the import is committed it writes one `[CRITICAL] [IMPORT]` line: `The database was replaced from an uploaded backup by chief (user 1 before the import): 2048.0 KB, SHA-256 <64 hex digits>, a backup this server made that nobody has changed, restored as it is. The system log was kept as it was; the backup's 4210 log line(s), 2026-10-01 00:00:00 to 2026-11-17 09:12:44, were not brought in and remain in the file.` The SHA-256 is the file's fingerprint (`Get-FileHash -Algorithm SHA256 <file>` or `sha256sum <file>`), so a backup held anywhere can be matched to the import. The middle part says `not a backup this server sealed` for a file with no seal this server can check, `a backup whose seal does not check out: changed since, or sealed by another server` for one changed since, or made elsewhere, and `a backup this server could not check, as it has no key to check a seal with` on a server that has never made a backup; the end says `restored as a working copy` for that choice. The log's first and last times are given only where both are real times; otherwise the line says `at times in no form this build reads`, and nothing the backup's log holds is ever quoted. A log in the file that cannot be read at all is said to be one `which could not be counted`, and the import goes ahead. The name and id are the account's as it was before the import, and the line has no user of its own, since after the import that id may be someone else's; its details give the address the import came from, and the check a damaged file failed. The line is written first, as soon as the restore commits: if anything fails after it, a second `CRITICAL` line says `Database restore committed, and then failed: …`, and the page says the backup was restored and committed. An import that changed nothing ends the line it writes with `(import by chief, user 1)`, with the account and address on the line: a file that is not a SQLite database and a backup with users and no superadmin each write one `[WARN] [IMPORT] Import refused: …` line, and a file that fails before the restore begins writes `[CRITICAL] [IMPORT] Database restore failed: …`. The result says the same of the log, last: "The system log was kept as it was: the backup's 4210 log line(s) were not brought in, and remain in the backup file." **A line's user after an import** is the account that had that id when the line was written; the restored data may give that id to someone else, and the Audit page's Live Console labels a line by today's account with that id, so read the name in the line's text.

- **It signs everybody out, you included.** No session is restored, whatever the backup holds, and the ones there were are cleared with everything else. When the result is dismissed the page reloads at the login page: sign in with an account the backup has, and its password as it was when the backup was made, or, where it was changed on this server since, the one it has now (card 2997). From a backup whose seal does not check out, only an account that is the same account here signs in with a password, the one it has on this server; where you are not in such a backup as the same account, its superadmin was given your own password and authenticators, so sign in as it with your password and a code from your authenticator (card 3009, below). The result names the backup's superadmin, which is the account that can export, import or reset again. Invites that had not been used stop working, and are sent again from User Management. Every browser remembered for sign-in is forgotten too, since no backup holds the key they are signed with: each is remembered again at its next sign-in.
- **Every request link mailed before it stops working** (cards 2158 and 2870). Every task request's links are ended, whatever the backup holds, and none is brought in; the result says so, counting the requests restored: "N request link(s) replaced: a link mailed before this restore no longer works, and its requester can have a new one sent from the page it opens." A requester who opens an old link is asked for the address they made the request with, and a new link is mailed to that address, if mail is set up here with its password; the next team reply or progress mail brings one by itself too. Until it is, the requester is told "New links cannot be sent just now. Please contact the team that sent you the link.", and the reason is a `[WARN] [REQUESTS]` line on the Live Console. The result shows the line about the links on the page too, below the files put back. The key the public request form signs with is not brought in either: a form open during the import fails once, and works when it is reloaded.
- **A backup made before card 2772's update gives its API keys this server's deadline.** Each of its keys with no expiry, or one later than the deadline, is given the date this server's keys were given (or, on a server that has none, the restore's own moment, so those keys end at once), as the update did here. The result says so after the request links' line, and on the page: "N API key(s) from before two-step sign-in were given this server's deadline, *date time* UTC." Their holders keep each with **Keep this key**, as in [The Release That Gives Older API Keys a Deadline](#the-release-that-gives-older-api-keys-a-deadline). A backup made since that update is restored as it is: a key kept there stays kept.
- **Every personal decision link stops working** (card 2675). Each one a decision participant held is cancelled, whatever the backup holds, and recorded on its decision where that decision has been opened. The result says how many (card 2697), after the request links' line and on the page too: "N personal decision link(s) cancelled: a link mailed before this restore no longer works, and the decision's author, decider or an administrator can send its holder a new one with Resend." It says so also when the restore committed and then failed; with none cancelled, there is no line. A backup that holds a decision's record with a row that cannot be copied is refused, naming the tables, and nothing is changed: half a record would be a false one.
- **This server's secrets are kept where they are for the same account, from a backup this server made and nobody has changed** (card 2169). A backup holds no mail password, destination credential or webhook secret, so the restore fills each gap from this server, in the same transaction, but only when the backup's seal checks out: every backup carries a check of every row in it, made with a key this server keeps and no backup holds, and the restore makes it again with its own key. A backup made by another server, or changed since it was made in anything a restore brings in (one user added, one card edited, an index added, ANALYZE run on it), has nothing filled and every gap listed. The check leaves out only what a restore never brings in: SQLite's hidden row numbers in a table that does not show them as a column (their order is checked), its AUTOINCREMENT counters, the file's header and its free space; so a change there changes nothing restored. The check covers the file's whole structure, SQLite's own objects and statistics included, and the restore copies each table's rows as stored, the same rows the check was made over. A file that fails SQLite's own integrity check, or holds an object named as SQLite's own that no backup of this server's holds, is treated as changed before the seal is even asked: the restore goes ahead, nothing is filled, and the system log has one `WARN` line naming the check it failed. When the seal checks out, the restore fills:
  - the mail password, when the backup's mail host (spaces and tabs at either end, and capitals A to Z, aside), port, "Allow mail without a checked, encrypted connection" and user name are all this server's, a port not set read as 587 and the box not set as off (card 2189: a backup made before it has no such setting);
  - a destination's credentials, from the one destination here of the same type that is the same in every other field, its name, host, bucket, folder or path included, compared as the destination would use them (the rule a save keeps a masked secret by: see [Configuring Backups](#configuring-backups)). None that matches, or more than one, and nothing is filled: a backup is not trusted to say where this server's credentials are used. A Google Drive token filled in brings this server's record of the scope it was granted, and an SFTP destination takes the host key confirmed here, never the backup's (card 2250): one not filled comes in with no key, to be confirmed with Test;
  - a webhook's secret, from the webhook here with the same id, board, address and setter (the account that last set its address or secret, or switched it on, card 2168; none and none alike), and the webhook is switched on or off as it was when the backup was made. A secret here set by another account since the backup is not filled, and the webhook is listed to enter again, as one whose address has changed is.

  **A restore never brings back an API key that was revoked or ended here** (card 2995). Every revoke, every ending that ends an account's keys (an administrator's password set, an invite used, your own change that ends your keys, a two-step reset in the app or on the host, a disable, a delete) and Reset Everything records the key's id and the fingerprint it is checked by, never the key. The record is this server's, as the system log is: an import never clears it, and leaves out of the restored data every key it names, whichever server made the backup and whichever way it is restored. Only a backup this server sealed adds its own record to it; one whose seal does not check out adds nothing (card 3013, below). Keys this server no longer held when the update that brought the record in first started are recorded by their id, up to the highest key number it had issued, so a backup made before that update brings none of them back either, a key that an earlier import removed included. The result says how many: "N API key(s) in the backup were left out, because each was revoked or ended on this server, was gone from it before endings were recorded, or is named by the record of ended keys of a backup imported here before, another server's among them: none of them works, and their holders make new ones on the Account page." The last case is the record failing closed: once another server's record of ended keys has been added here (by a release before card 3013, which added an unchecked backup's record), it stays here, and a later import can leave out a key of this server's that only shares an id with one that server ended; it is counted, and made again in a minute. The `[CRITICAL] [IMPORT]` line ends its first sentence `restored as it is, leaving out N API key(s) ended on this server`, its details naming them by id and no account. Their holders make new keys on the Account page. What such a key set up, or was given the secret of, is held as it is when a key is revoked. A key made here after the backup was made is not in it and is gone after the import, as before: it was not ended, so importing a newer backup brings it back. **A restore onto another server cannot know of keys ended on that other server after the backup was made**: it applies its own record, nothing else, since that server's backup never checks out there and adds none of its record (card 3013). And a key ended before the update that brought the record in, whose fingerprint this server never saw, can come back if someone edits an old backup to move it to a key number the record does not name: only the superadmin can import, and the import's line names the file's SHA-256. Should a key the record names ever be found in place all the same (an older image's import, a hand edit), every check of a key refuses it, and the next start, or the sweep within five minutes, ends it as a revoke does, with one `[WARN] [AUTH]` line naming it by id.

  **A restore never brings back an account deleted, disabled or demoted here** (card 2996). Every delete, disable, enable and role change of an account, the role it was made with, and Reset Everything's disable of every account but the superadmin, are recorded by the account's number and creation time; a delete also by a fingerprint of the password hash it held. The record is this server's: an import never clears it, and only a backup this server sealed adds its own to it, within the backup's own bounds, never taking a row that would end or lower a superadmin; one whose seal does not check out adds nothing (card 3013, below). Accounts this server no longer held when the update that brought the record in first started are recorded by their number, up to the highest it had issued. On every import, as it is or as a working copy:

  - an account the record names as **deleted** comes back disabled by the superadmin, with no password or second factor that works, and cannot be enabled: delete it again on the Users page, then make a new account for anyone who returns;
  - an account that shares a number with one this server had **before it recorded endings** (or held, just before the import, with a later creation time) comes back disabled with no password that works: if it should stay, the superadmin enables it and sends it an invite;
  - an account the record keeps **disabled** stays disabled, by the superadmin; a backup can add a disable to an account, never lift one made here;
  - an account above the **role** the record holds is given back that lower role, and the webhooks it could read and no longer can are held.

  None of them gets the backup's second factors, and none is named in the result's second-factor lines. What the account could send out goes as when it is ended here: its API keys are left out and counted, its webhooks switched off or held, its report schedules paused, and the settings it set and its address in the backup failure address go once the import is done (those `WARN` lines name the setter, as they always have). The result counts them and names nobody, one line for each kind there was: "N account(s) in the backup are named as deleted by the record of account endings: each is kept disabled, with no password or second factor that works, and cannot be enabled again. Delete each on the Users page before making a new account for anyone who returns: its username and email address would otherwise block the new one.", "N account(s) in the backup share a number with an account this server had before it recorded account endings: …", "N account(s) in the backup are kept disabled, as the record of account endings says." and "N account(s) in the backup were given back the lower role the record of account endings holds." The `[CRITICAL] [IMPORT]` line says `restored as it is, keeping N account(s) ended or lowered as the record of account endings says`, with the counts in its details and no account. **A restore onto another server applies its own record, nothing else**: that server's backup never checks out there, so its record is not taken (card 3013), and it cannot know of accounts ended on that other server after the backup was made. An account found otherwise all the same (an older image's import, a host edit) is ended again at the next start, or within five minutes, with one `[WARN] [AUTH]` line of counts.

  **A restore never brings back a password replaced here** (card 2997). Every password change, your own on the Account page, an administrator's 🔑, an invite used and Reset Everything, records a fingerprint of the password hash it replaces (never the hash, and not something a password can be checked against) in the same record of account endings, which no import clears. On every import, of this server's backups or another's, as it is or as a working copy, an account whose password in the backup is one the record names as replaced:

  - **that is the same account here** (the same number and creation time) keeps the password it has on this server, and the one in the backup does not work; it keeps this server's second factors, as the same account always does;
  - **that is not here as the same account**, or holds here a password that cannot be given back (one nothing matches, or one the record names too), is given a password nothing matches and no leave to set up two-step sign-in on it; its API keys are left out and counted, what they could read is held, and its report schedules are paused, as a reset does; and it needs its password set (🔑) or an invite (📧) before it can sign in. It is not disabled: nothing says it was ended;
  - **that is a superadmin** is never left unable to sign in. The same account here keeps the password it has here, even one the record names too. A superadmin not here keeps the backup's password, unless you, importing, are the same superadmin here with a password that works: then it is given one nothing matches wherever any account's replaced password is the one it holds, as any other account is. A superadmin given one nothing matches can sign in again only once another superadmin sets its password (🔑): no invite is sent for a superadmin. Sign in as a superadmin left with such a password and change it on the Account page at once.

  A backup whose seal does not check out (made by another server, changed since, or this server's own made before Reset Everything, before the database was lost, or before card 2943's key) is trusted less: every account in it that is the same account here keeps the password it has on this server, whatever the backup holds and whatever the record says, and every account in it that is not takes no password from it at all (card 3009, below). The result counts them and names nobody, one line for each kind there was: "N account(s) in the backup hold a password that was changed on this server after the backup was made, or, where the backup's seal did not verify, any other than the one each has on this server: each keeps the password it has on this server, and the one in the backup does not work.", "N account(s) in the backup hold a password that was replaced on this server, or, where the backup's seal did not verify, any other than the one each has on this server, and have no password on this server that can be given back to them: none of those passwords works, and each account needs its password set (🔑) or an invite (📧; for a superadmin, 🔑 by another superadmin) before it can sign in." and "N superadmin account(s) were left with a password that was replaced on this server, or, where the backup's seal did not verify, one other than this server's, so that a superadmin can still sign in: sign in and change it on the Account page at once." The `[CRITICAL] [IMPORT]` line says `keeping N of the backup's password(s) out of use` and `leaving N superadmin account(s) with a password to change at once`, each only where there was any, with the counts in its details and no account. A restore that committed and then failed says the same. Should such a password be found in place all the same (an older image's import, a host file rollback, a host edit), a sign-in with it is answered as a wrong password, no session is made with it, and the password asked again before an action refuses it the same way; the next start, or the sweep within five minutes, gives the account a password nothing matches and signs it out, with one `[WARN] [AUTH]` line of counts. A superadmin is never refused or changed this way: a `[WARN] [AUTH]` line at every start and sweep asks for its password to be changed, until it is.

  **What it cannot catch.** A password changed before the release that brought this in was never fingerprinted, so a backup made before that change can still bring it back: after restoring such a backup, reset those accounts' passwords as before. A backup whose seal does not check out no longer gives an account that is not here any password it holds (card 3009, below, with what that cannot catch). A forged backup's rows naming passwords in use here as replaced are no longer taken: a backup whose seal does not check out adds nothing to the record (card 3013, below), and a backup this server sealed cannot be forged without its seal key.

  **A backup whose seal does not check out gives nothing that signs in to an account that is not the same account here** (card 3009). Such a file is only the uploader's word: another server's backup, an edited one, one made before seals, or this server's own made before the backup seal key was made anew (card 2943). Every backup made since card 3009 carries this installation's id (32 hex characters, not secret, never brought in by a restore), so an account is **the same account here** when it has the same number and creation time and either the backup is sealed by this server, or it carries this installation's id, or it carries none (made before card 3009) and the username is exactly the same. A backup carrying another installation's id has no account that is the same here. From such a file, as it is or as a working copy:

  - **an account that is not the same here** takes no password, second factor or API key from it: it is given a password nothing matches and no leave to set up two-step sign-in, its report schedules are paused (switched off with every other on a working copy), whatever password the file holds for it, a password nothing matches included, and it stays enabled, needing 🔑 or 📧. Its keys are left out and counted, never recorded as ended here (they never were this server's);
  - **no invite of the file** is restored at all, and from a backup this server sealed only used ones, as history, written used whatever the file says (its code review, FP1): no invite from a file can set anybody's password;
  - **an account that is the same here** keeps this server's password, factors and keys, and a key it keeps takes the earlier of this server's and the backup's expiry and deadline, so a key expired here never comes back working; a key the backup holds that this server did not hold (the same id, fingerprint and owner) is left out, an account here or not;
  - **a superadmin of this server that is the same account here** is a superadmin after the import, whatever the file says of it, disabled only where it is disabled here; since card 3013, every account of this server's number and creation time takes this server's role, so no file raises one (below);
  - **the backup's superadmin, where you are not in the backup as the same account and nobody can sign in as the superadmin afterwards**: the lowest-numbered enabled superadmin of the file that is not the same here is given your own password, your authenticators sealed again for it (not your recovery codes), your leave to set up two-step sign-in on your password alone in place of the file's, and your email address, always: any other account in the backup holding that address is given one that receives no mail (`email-given-up-<id>@restore.invalid`), counted, and needs its own set on the Users page (its code review, FP5, FP6). Its report schedules, the file's, are paused. Every other such superadmin gets a password nothing matches. Sign in as it, the superadmin that now holds your email address, with your password and a code from your authenticator. Where no MFA key is loaded, or none of your authenticators could be moved, it signs in only once it has set up an authenticator on that password (where you held that leave and its 14 days still run), or once the host has run `scripts/mfa-reset.mjs` for it. Where you, or any other superadmin of this server restored as the same account, enabled and holding a password of this server's, can sign in afterwards, or none of them is enabled, or the import was not by an account this server held, every such superadmin gets a password nothing matches. Where nobody on this server had a second factor (a backup made before two-step sign-in), your authenticators are the first, and the result says that every other account now needs one (FP12);
  - **the mail settings, the App URL and new backup destinations** are held for review, when restored as it is (see [Settings Held for Review](#settings-held-for-review-card-2922)); a working copy removes them, the App URL included;
  - **every webhook of the file that has an address** is switched off for good and its secret discarded, whatever the file says of it, as it is or as a working copy (card 3012): its address, secret and setter are only the file's word. It sends nothing until an owner of its board or an administrator switches it on with `PUT /api/v1/webhooks/{id}` and `{"active": true}` alone, which makes them its setter and gives it a new secret, given in that answer; a secret sent with it is refused. Put the new secret in at its receiver. Delete it instead unless you recognise the address. A webhook with no address that was on is switched off and listed to enter, as before (from a backup made before card 2923's release it is then held too, as every webhook already off is: switching it on gives it a new secret). Where this server holds the same webhook, held, with the same address, it keeps this server's reason. The restore's notice of the webhooks it switched off or held is written to the log at `WARN` and mailed to nobody;
  - **every report schedule that was on** is paused, when restored as it is (a working copy switches every one off already): each owner turns one on again on the **Reports** page, with their password and a code;
  - **an account that is the same here** keeps the email address and notification choices it has on this server; any other account of the file holding one of those addresses (in any case) is given one that receives no mail (`email-given-up-<id>@restore.invalid`), as is every account not the same here that shares an address with a lower-numbered one apart from case, and any account not the same here whose address the file put under `@restore.invalid` (with a code of the restore's in it): set each one's address on the Users page before inviting it;
  - **every account not the same here**, the superadmin given your password included, has its notification mail switched off, its other choices kept, until its holder signs in and switches it on (Account page). It keeps the file's email address (card 3021, below);
  - **the backup failure mail** goes to this server's address (Scheduled Backups), not the backup's, or to none where this server has none;
  - **the mail's From Address and From Name** are this server's, exactly as they were, not the backup's, or none where this server had none (card 3029), restored as it is; a working copy removes them with the rest of the mail settings. Where none is left (a move to a server whose mail was not set up), set both under 📧 Email / SMTP before anything is mailed: until then a mail goes with no From and the envelope `MAIL FROM:<>`, which most mail servers refuse;
  - **every task request with an address that this server did not hold as it is** has its mail to the requester held, as it is or as a working copy (card 3020): the same request here means the same id, made at the same moment, with the same title, requester address, requester account, status and card, the card with the same title and creation and on the same board, and the requester's account the same here. A held request is sent no progress mail, completion report, accept or decline, reply or new link until an administrator releases it under **Mail held** in the Requests inbox ([Mail Held in the Requests Inbox](#mail-held-in-the-requests-inbox-card-3020)). An unconfirmed one cannot be released and lapses 7 days after it was made. A request this server held as it is keeps this server's hold, or none; the file's own hold text is never taken;
  - **every person in the people directory whose address and account this server did not hold** (the same id, the same address, the same account, and that account the same here), or whose status in the file is neither active nor left, is marked **Left** at the moment of the restore, and one this server has as left stays left with this server's date (card 3020). Each is recorded in the people change record by DumpFire, source `restore`, with no address. A person who has left is mailed nothing about a decision, so their places on decisions are mailed nothing; nothing in the decisions' records is rewritten. Check each address, then **Mark active**, with your password and a code ([People](#people)).

  The result counts them and names nobody: "N account(s) in the backup are not on this server as the same account, and the backup's seal did not verify, so the password each holds in it was not taken: each needs its password set (🔑) or an invite (📧) before it can sign in, and its report schedules were paused. The backup's App URL and mail host, where they differ from this server's, are held for review. Before sending an invite, check each one's email address and role, and the App URL and the mail host: in a backup whose seal did not verify they are only the file's word. Or set its password with 🔑, which mails nothing." (as a working copy: "… each needs its password set (🔑) before it can sign in. As a working copy, every report schedule was switched off, and the mail settings and the backup's App URL were removed, so no invite can be mailed. …"), "The backup's superadmin is not the account you imported with, and the backup's seal did not verify, so its password in the backup was not taken: it was given the password you confirmed this import with, and your authenticators. Sign in as it, the superadmin that now holds your email address, …" (never a username, which is the file's text), "N API key(s) in the backup were left out, because the backup's seal did not verify and this server did not hold them for the same account: …" and the held settings' line. The `[CRITICAL] [IMPORT]` line counts them among `keeping N of the backup's password(s) out of use`, adds `giving the backup's superadmin the importing account's password` and `N API key(s) of a backup whose seal did not verify`, and its details say only whether the backup's installation id is this one's (`this`, `other` or `none`), never the id. **Check each account's email address and role, and the App URL and mail host, before sending any invite**: in such a file they are only its word, and 🔑 mails nothing. The 📧 confirm now names the address it will send to, "Send {username} a new invite, to {address}? …", with any control, direction or invisible character shown as a space (card 3012).

  What it kept from reaching outside (card 3012) is counted in one more sentence, each part only where there was any: "The backup's seal did not verify, so what it would send outside was not taken as the file has it: N webhook(s) with an address were held and their secrets discarded: each sends nothing until an owner of its board or an administrator switches it on, which gives it a new secret; N report schedule(s) were paused: each owner turns one on again on the Reports page; N account(s) that are the same here keep the email address and notification choices they have on this server; N other account(s) that held one of those addresses were given one that receives no mail: set each one's address on the Users page before inviting it; N account(s) not the same here have their notification mail switched off until each signs in and switches it on; N request(s) came in with a requester or card this server did not hold as it is: mail to each requester, links and completion reports included, is held until an administrator releases it under Mail held in the Requests inbox; N unconfirmed request(s) were held too: they cannot be released, and lapse 7 days after they were made; N person(s) in the people directory were marked left, as this server did not hold their address and account, or has them as left, so N place(s) on decisions are mailed nothing: an administrator marks each active on the People page, with a password and a code, once the address is checked; mail goes out under this server's From address and name, not the backup's, or under none where this server had none; and the backup failure mail goes to this server's address, not the backup's." (card 3020 added the requests and people clauses, card 3029 the From). The webhooks held are also named by id and board in the "To enter again" line, as switched off for good. The `[CRITICAL] [IMPORT]` line adds `keeping what a backup whose seal did not verify would send outside: …` with the same counts (card 3020's as `N request(s)' requester mail held`, `N unconfirmed request(s) held`, `N person(s) marked left` and `N place(s) on decisions mailed nothing`, and card 3029's as `the mail's From address and name kept as on this server`), and no username, address, host, schedule name, request title, person's name or id.

  **What it cannot catch** (card 3009). A backup that carries no installation id (made before card 3009) is matched by number, creation time and username, so two servers built by one script, with the same usernames made in the same seconds, are taken for each other. A superadmin renamed since such a backup was made is not the same account in an unsealed restore of it, and needs the host's `scripts/mfa-reset.mjs`. A password of the same account that differs from this server's is counted with card 2997's lines above, and an account not the same here with card 3009's, apart. And a backup of a copy of this server's volume or host file (the same seal key and installation id) checks out as this server's own: see [Deployment](deployment.md#moving-to-a-new-host). Card 3012 does not cover invites and notices to an account not the same here, which still go to the file's address (card 3021): check those addresses before anything mails them. A request's requester and the people of such a file are card 3020's (above); a backup made before card 2095 normalised timestamps holds older-form creation times, so its first unsealed restore holds those requests too, which fails closed: review and release them. Webhooks and schedules switched off or deleted here coming back from a sealed backup are card 3004's, and board or team access an unsealed file grants is card 3002's.

  **A backup whose seal does not check out adds nothing to this server's records of endings, and changes no account here** (card 3013). Anyone holding a backup holds every account's number, creation time and password fingerprint, and every key's, so such a file could plant endings that reset or end every account and key here, and a later restore of a genuine backup would apply them again; it could also make an account that is the same here a superadmin, which then signed in with its own password. From such a file, as it is or as a working copy:

  - **neither record takes a row from it**: its record of account endings and its record of ended API keys are counted and not added, and neither id counter moves on its word;
  - **no safety entry is written from it**: an account it makes inert or disabled is ended for this restore, but no `deleted`, `disabled` or `password` row is written from the password the file chose; one this server's own record names is ended again at the next start;
  - **only keys this server held are recorded**: where an account of the file is ended, the keys this server held for that same account (the same id, fingerprint and owner) go on the record of ended keys; every other key is deleted unrecorded, and no planted key's fingerprint is recorded (card 3014);
  - **every account of this server's number and creation time takes this server's role and enabled or disabled state exactly**, a superadmin's included, so no file raises or lowers an account here, nor leaves a disable for a later genuine restore to carry; where that leaves no enabled superadmin, the import is refused and nothing changes;
  - **an account is judged by this server's password where it is the same here**, and by none where it is not, and only this server's superadmins are spared the record's rows; a disable here is carried only where this server's record attests it.

  On every restore, sealed or not, an account counts as one this server held with a later creation time only where this server's record holds a row for it. The result counts what was put back and not taken, and names nobody, each sentence only where its count is above 0: "N account(s) in the backup that are on this server took the role and the enabled or disabled state they have here, not the backup's: the backup's seal did not verify, so neither is taken from it." and "The backup's seal did not verify, so its record of N account ending(s) and of M ended API key(s) was not added to this server's: a file this server cannot check ends no account and no key here, and this server's own records are kept as they were." The panel shows both. The `[CRITICAL] [IMPORT]` line adds `giving N account(s) this server's role and enabled or disabled state` and `adding none of the backup's N account ending(s) and M ended API key(s) to this server's records, as its seal did not verify`, its details carrying `accountStateAsHere` and `recordsNotTaken: { accountEndings, keyRevocations }`. None of it is a problem: an import of this server's own backup made before Reset Everything reads as a clean restore, with nothing in its list of problems.

  **What it cannot catch** (card 3013). A move by import onto another server brings neither record, and nor does a restore of this server's own backup onto a database rebuilt after a loss, since the seal key went with the old database: both records start empty, and, by card 3009, no password, second factor or key that signs in comes back. Move the host instead where you can ([Moving to a New Host](deployment.md#moving-to-a-new-host)). An older backup of another server brings its ended accounts back enabled but unable to sign in, with the file's role: check each before inviting it. And an account an unchecked restore disabled only because this server held its number with a later creation time is left disabled with a password nothing matches, and nothing re-asserts it: an administrator could enable it and set a password, deliberately.

  **A restore never brings back board, team or person access removed here** (card 3002). Every change of who may open a board (adding or removing a member, changing a role, sharing it with a team or taking the share away, making it public or private, and its creator), of who is in a team, of a board's Product Owner, and of the account a person in the people directory is linked to, is recorded by the identity of each side (number and creation time), never a name. The record is this server's: an import never clears it, and only a backup this server sealed adds its own rows to it, rows that can narrow access and never lift it. On every import, as it is or as a working copy:

  - a member or share removed here since the backup is left out, and a role lowered here is given back the lower role;
  - a board made private here stays private; a Product Owner or creator replaced here is cleared, never replaced (set the Product Owner again on the board's Share window if needed);
  - a person unlinked here stays unlinked, from a backup this server sealed;
  - a team deleted here comes back, but without the members and board shares it had then: delete it again on the Teams page if it is not wanted. A member added after it came back stays;
  - access this server holds when the import runs is never taken away on a guess: a member row with no record of its own (made on an older image, or by a host edit) is kept at the access it has here, and is left out only where the backup holds it and this server did not.

  **From a backup whose seal does not check out**, who may open what is not taken from the file: every account that is the same here gets exactly the board and team access it has here, none on the boards and teams this server does not hold, and those boards come back private, open to administrators only until shared again. A person's account link is kept only where both the person and the account are the same here; a person whose link the file changed has this server's link back and stays marked **Left** until an administrator marks them active, with a password and a code. Nothing of the file's record of access changes is added.

  The result counts each kind and names nobody, each sentence only where there was any: "N board or team membership(s) and share(s) in the backup were removed on this server after it was made, and are left out.", "N role(s) on boards and teams were given back the lower role they have on this server.", "N board(s) made private on this server after the backup stay private.", "N board(s) keep no Product Owner, and N no recorded creator, as this server's record of access says …", "N team(s) deleted on this server came back without the members and boards they had then …", "N person(s) unlinked from an account on this server since the backup stay unlinked." and, from a backup whose seal does not check out, "The backup's seal did not verify, so who may open what is not taken from it: …". The `[CRITICAL] [IMPORT]` line says `keeping N access change(s) made on this server` and, unchecked, `giving N board(s), team(s) and person(s) this server's access`, with the counts in its details. The next start, and every five minutes, lowers again any recorded access found above its record (an older image's import, a host edit), with one `[WARN] [AUTH]` line of counts.

  **What it cannot catch** (card 3002). Boards and teams deleted before the update that brought the record in are not recorded, and come back whole, with the access they had, from an older backup that holds them; so does a board whose creator's creation time, or a person whose linked account's, does not read as a moment. A board, team or person whose own creation time does not read is given the restore's moment by the restore that brings it, with one line counting them, and is recorded from then on. Access changed by a host edit, or while an older version ran, is not recorded either: send it again from the Share window or the team's page to record it.

  **A restore never brings back a decision role removed here** (card 3033). The same record holds every change of a decision's participants, of the account each place holds and of its decider. On every import, as it is or as a working copy, from a backup this server sealed:

  - a participant taken off a decision here since the backup stays off it (on a draft the place is deleted again where nothing names it), and one made informed stays informed;
  - an account taken off a place stays off it: attach it again in the Delivery panel if needed;
  - a decider replaced here is cleared, its name kept; the author, a board owner or an administrator names one again, except on a decision that supersedes another or has no board, which cannot be given one: withdraw it if it is not wanted;
  - a decision deleted here comes back with no author, decider or participants: delete it again if it is not wanted. A participant or decider added after it came back stays;
  - a role this server holds when the import runs is never taken away on a guess.

  **From a backup whose seal does not check out**, no decision role is taken from the file. Every decision this server holds keeps its board, its stage (a decision past its draft is never sent back to one, and a draft is never opened by the file), its author, decider and participants exactly as here; a place the file adds is taken off it. Where the file changes the decision's board itself (its creation time, say), so that the board is no longer the same one here, the decision comes in with no board and, as one this server does not hold, with no author or decider account: their names are kept, and an administrator names a decider again or withdraws it. Every decision this server does not hold comes in with no board, no account on it and nobody on it, so only an administrator can open it (withdrawing it mails nobody). No ruling's Product Owner is taken from the file: each ruling keeps this server's, or none. On boards this server does not hold, member rows, creators and Product Owners naming an account the backup does not bring are cleared, and no account made later can take a number a restored row names. On every import, a restored account number that is not a whole number from 1 to 2^53 - 1 is cleared (its row left out where the row cannot be without it), with one line counting them: "N restored account number(s) that are not a whole number from 1 to 2^53 - 1 were cleared, or their rows left out where one is needed, so that no account made here can take them."

  The result adds, each only where there was any: "N place(s) on decisions removed on this server after the backup was made stay removed.", "N participant(s) made informed on this server stay informed.", "N account(s) taken off a place on a decision on this server stay off it: …", "N decision(s) keep no decider, as this server's record says: …", "N decision(s) keep no decider and cannot be given one here, …", "N decision(s) deleted on this server came back with no author, decider or participants: …" and, unchecked, "… and of the decisions in it, N this server holds kept their board, stage, author, decider and participants as here, and N it does not hold came in with no board, no account and nobody on them." The `[CRITICAL] [IMPORT]` line counts them among the access changes kept and, unchecked, says `N decision(s) no role this server did not give`. Counts only, naming no decision or person. The next start, and every five minutes, lowers again any recorded role found above its record.

  **What it cannot catch** (card 3033). Decisions made, and roles changed, while an older version ran or by a host edit are not recorded: make each removal again on this release. A decision whose creation time does not read as a moment is matched by its number alone while any row of the record has that number, and is not tracked otherwise.

  **A restore never brings back where this server stopped sending** (card 3004). Every change of where this server sends is recorded by identity, never as a name or an address in plain: a webhook (by its number and creation time, its address as a keyed digest) made, switched off or on, given another address, held by an ending, or deleted on its own or with its board; a report schedule made, switched off or on, paused by an ending, or deleted on its own, with its board or with its account, and each address added to it or taken off it (a keyed digest); and a backup destination, the mail server and the App URL set, moved, held or removed (by place). The record is this server's: an import and Reset Everything keep it, and only a backup this server sealed adds its own rows to it, rows that can close and never open. On every import, as it is or as a working copy:

  - **nothing comes back more open than this server holds it**: a webhook or schedule switched off here stays off, a webhook held here stays held with this server's reason whatever address or setter the backup gives it, and an address not on a schedule here is left off it;
  - **a webhook deleted here** (on its own or with its board) comes back switched off for good, its secret discarded: "This was switched off because it was deleted on this server after the backup it came back from was made." Delete it again, or switch it on with your own key, which gives it a new secret;
  - **a webhook pointed elsewhere here** comes back switched off for good the same way ("… because it was pointed elsewhere on this server after the backup it came back from was made."); so does every webhook held here with an address when no MFA key is loaded, as its address cannot then be compared;
  - **a schedule deleted here** comes back paused, and **an address taken off a schedule here** stays off it; the schedule's owner turns it on again, or adds the address again, with a password and a code;
  - **a destination, the mail server or the App URL removed or moved here** comes back held for review under Settings held for review, "removed here" (see [Settings Held for Review](#settings-held-for-review-card-2922)); one held here by an ending comes back held as "setter access ended";
  - **from this server's own backup** (its seal verified), a webhook, schedule, destination, mail server or App URL that the record has never named and this server does not hold is taken as removed: switched off for good, paused or held, "not in this server's record". The addresses on a schedule are never taken off on a guess;
  - **an account the restore itself signs out**, because its password was replaced here, has the webhooks it set, and those whose secret it could read as the board's owner or an administrator, held as "setter access ended", and the settings it set held for review, as a real password reset holds them, from the backup's record of who set what and from this server's.

  The mail settings, the backup configuration and the App URL are read only as text (card 3031): a backup that holds one as raw bytes has it left out (a number is written as its text), with one line in the list of problems naming the keys and never the values, and no mail, backup run or link uses a value that is not text, here either.

  The result counts and names nothing, each sentence only where its count is above 0: "N webhook(s) deleted on this server after the backup was made came back switched off for good, their secrets discarded: delete them again, or switch one on with your own key to give it a new secret.", "N webhook(s) pointed elsewhere on this server since the backup was made came back switched off for good, their secrets discarded.", "N webhook(s) whose address could not be compared with this server's record (no MFA key is loaded, or it was replaced since the address was recorded) came back switched off for good, their secrets discarded.", "N webhook(s) and N report schedule(s) not in this server's record came back switched off or paused.", "N webhook(s) and N report schedule(s) switched off, paused or held on this server since the backup was made stay off.", "N report schedule(s) deleted on this server since the backup was made came back paused.", "N address(es) taken off report schedules on this server since the backup was made were left off.", "N report schedule(s) came back paused: addresses taken off them on this server were recorded under an MFA key since replaced, or none is loaded, so the addresses they came back with could not be compared.", "N report schedule(s) came back with more than 10 addresses: each was paused, and the N address(es) beyond the first 10 were left out.", "N setting(s) where this server sends (backup destinations, the mail settings or the App URL) removed, moved, held, or not in this server's record, are held for the superadmin's review under Settings held for review.", "N setting(s) set by accounts this restore signed out are held for the superadmin's review." and "N setting(s) where this server sends were not text in the backup, and were left out or read as text." From a backup whose seal does not check out, card 3013's sentence counts "N change(s) of where this server sends" among the rows not added. The `[CRITICAL] [IMPORT]` line adds `keeping N outbound change(s) made on this server`, and `holding N setting(s) set by accounts it signed out` and `leaving out or reading as text N outbound setting(s) that were not text` where any (and, apart, `holding N webhook(s) and pausing N report schedule(s) whose addresses could not be compared with this server's record` and `pausing N report schedule(s) over 10 addresses and leaving out N address(es)`), its details carrying `outboundKept`. The next start, and every five minutes, holds again any recorded webhook, schedule, address or setting found above its record (an older image's import, a host edit): webhooks told and mailed as an ending's are, the rest in one `[WARN] [BACKUP]` line of counts.

  **What it cannot catch** (card 3004). Changes made while an older version ran, or by a host edit, are not recorded: make each removal again on this release. A webhook or schedule deleted before this release is not in the record: from this server's own backup it comes back held or paused as "not in this server's record", and from a backup whose seal does not check out as card 3012 holds it. After the MFA key is replaced, the recorded addresses no longer match, so a restore holds those webhooks as "re-pointed here" (failing closed). The backup schedule, Keep Last, the backup failure mail's address and a schedule's status filter still come back as the backup has them (their own cards).

  **A restore keeps the webhooks and report schedules a reset switched off** (card 3004). A backup made before a password reset holds the account's webhooks and report schedules as they were then; since card 3004, restored as it is, a webhook the reset held stays held and a schedule it paused stays paused, as the record says. The account's API keys do not come back (above), and nor does the password the reset replaced (card 2997). From a backup whose seal does not check out, every webhook with an address comes back held and every schedule paused as well (card 3012, above).

  A value the backup has is never replaced, so a backup made before this change restores as it always did. Restored on the server it came from, nothing is to enter, unless a destination's name, folder, bucket or path, or the mail port or "Allow mail without a checked, encrypted connection", has been changed since the backup. The result adds up to two lines, only when there is something to say: "Kept from this server, because a backup does not hold them: …" and "To enter again, because a backup does not hold them: …", which names the mail password (Admin, 📧 Email / SMTP), each destination with what it needs (Admin, Scheduled Backups) and each webhook with its board. A restore brings in "Allow mail without a checked, encrypted connection" with the backup's other mail settings, with no password asked and no `WARN` line; where the backup's differs from this server's, the mail password is not kept and the line names the box: "the mail password (Admin, 📧 Email / SMTP, where the backup brought in "Allow mail without a checked, encrypted connection" ticked and this server had it unticked)", or "unticked and this server had it ticked". Look at the box before typing the password. For a backup whose seal did not check out, or whose file failed a check, the second line ends "None was kept from this server, because this backup was not made by this server, or was changed since."; for one with no seal this server can check (made before seals, or restored on a server that has never made a backup), it ends "None was kept from this server, because this backup carries no seal this server can check." A webhook named there stays switched off until the board's owner or an administrator sets its secret and switches it on with `PUT /api/v1/webhooks/{id}` and `{ "secret": "…", "active": true }`; one named as switched off for good (held because the backup's seal did not verify, card 3012) is switched on with `{ "active": true }` alone, which gives it a new secret and refuses one sent with it. The second line is also written to the system log at `WARN`, and from then on every start of the server writes one `[WARN] [STARTUP]` line while anything on it is still to enter.
- **Second factors are kept for the same accounts, and an account left without one is named** (card 2768). An account that is the same one here and in the backup (the same id and the same creation time, whatever its username now; from a backup whose seal does not check out, also carrying this installation's id, or, where it carries none, the same username: card 3009, above) keeps this server's second factors and recovery codes as they are, so a factor removed since the backup stays removed and a code used since stays used. Any other account gets the backup's only when the backup is this server's own, unchanged (its seal), and its factors were made with this server's key; otherwise it gets none. Every account's count of wrong codes and its pauses start again from nothing. Sign-ins waiting for a second factor and links to set up an authenticator are never brought in. Once two-step sign-in is in use (a factor exists after the restore, or the backup's were left out), the result names every account left with no second factor, on the page after the secrets' lines and in the log at `WARN`: "N account(s) have no second factor after this restore and each needs its two-step sign-in reset before it can sign in: ann, bob." From a backup whose seal does not check out, no account is named, the file's usernames being only its word (card 3009; its code review, FP7): the accounts that are the same here are counted, "N account(s) on this server have no second factor after this restore and each needs its two-step sign-in reset before it can sign in; the backup's seal did not verify, so none is named here.", and the others apart, "N account(s) in the backup are not on this server as the same account and have no second factor after this restore: each needs its two-step sign-in reset before it can sign in." When the backup's factors for any account were left out, it goes on to say why, in plain words: "Their second factors in the backup were left out because the backup was not made by this server, or was changed after it was made: bob." (or "because no MFA key is loaded on this server", or "because the backup was made under a different MFA key"). When any account's factors and codes were taken from the backup (an account this server never held, say: since card 2996 an account deleted, or disabled, here since the backup gets none of the backup's, and is named nowhere), it adds once: "Accounts brought back from the backup have their recovery codes as they were then; ask them to regenerate their codes." A code such an account used after the backup was made works again until it does. The result ends "See docs/admin-guide.md, Restoring from Backup." **What to do:** reset the two-step sign-in of each account named with 🛡️ (card 2773, see [Resetting Two-Step Sign-In](#resetting-two-step-sign-in); the superadmin's from the host), give its holder the link, and ask them to set up their authenticator again; ask each account brought back to regenerate its recovery codes. If you expected those accounts to keep their factors, check that the file is a backup this server made and nobody has changed, and that the server runs with the key the factors were made with (`MFA_KEY_FILE`: see [Deployment](deployment.md#the-key-that-protects-authenticator-secrets)). Until card 2769 lets accounts set up a second factor, nobody has one and the result says nothing about them.

  **Second factors, passwords and API keys survive a restore only onto the installation that made the backup.** The key a backup is sealed with belongs to the installation that made it and is in no backup, and so does its installation id. Restored onto a rebuilt server (a new volume, a new installation), a backup's seal does not check out and no account in it is the same account there, so every account in it is left with no second factor, **even when the server runs with the right MFA key**, and, since card 3009, with no password and no API key from it: each needs its two-step sign-in reset (card 2773) and its password set (🔑) or an invite (📧), and makes new keys; the restore counts them. The backup's superadmin is given the password and authenticators of whoever imported it (above). Plan a rebuild with that in mind: keep the database volume, with the MFA key file kept apart from it, if they are to survive (see [Moving to a New Host](deployment.md#moving-to-a-new-host)).

  **Backups made before the release that makes the backup seal key anew count as another server's** (card 2943). The first start of that release replaces the key this server seals its backups with, because the key before it was stored in plain and is in every whole copy of the database made before then. From then on, restoring a backup made before that start, even the one taken just before the update, works as restoring another server's backup does: everything is restored, but the mail password, each destination's credentials and each webhook's secret and address are listed to enter again, and accounts not the same here get no second factor, password or API key from it (card 3009). Such a backup carries no installation id either, so an account in it is the same here by its number, creation time and username: one renamed since needs 🔑 or 📧, and a superadmin renamed since the host's `scripts/mfa-reset.mjs`. Keep the mail password and the destinations' credentials to hand for that. Backups made after the start restore as before. The same happens again whenever the key is made anew: see [The Signing Keys Made Anew Once](authentication-security.md#the-signing-keys-made-anew-once-card-2943). Each time, the system log keeps one `CRITICAL` line beginning "Key change recorded", naming each key made and why. One is expected at a new installation's first start too, naming the backup seal key with "(none was stored)": that is the key being made, not a fault.
- **A backup with users and no superadmin among them is refused**, before anything is changed: "This backup has user accounts and none of them is the superadmin." Restoring it would leave nobody able to export, import or reset the database. A backup with no users at all is accepted: DumpFire then asks to be set up, and whoever does it is the superadmin. A backup that has a superadmin whose row cannot be copied is refused the same way, after the copy and before it is committed: the restore is rolled back and nothing is changed.
- **A backup that is too large says so.** See [When an Import Is Not Accepted](#when-an-import-is-not-accepted).
- **It takes a `.db` backup only.** The file picker offers `.db`, `.sqlite` and `.sqlite3` files, and the page refuses any other file itself, sending nothing. The JSON export DumpFire wrote in its first week, from 1 to 7 April 2026, is no longer imported: its import left twelve tables of the data it replaced in place, so that a live API key could then answer as an imported account. If such a file is ever wanted, import it into an empty installation of an image built before 14 October 2026 and export a `.db` from there.

The dialog asks how to restore, every time:

| Choice | For | What it does |
|--------|-----|--------------|
| **Restore everything as it is** | Putting this installation back | Backup destinations, report schedules, mail settings and webhooks come back as the backup has them. A backup holds no mail password, destination credential or webhook secret: the ones this server has for the same accounts are kept, and the result lists any to enter again; a webhook that had a secret stays off until it is set. From a backup whose seal does not check out, the mail settings, App URL and destinations at new places are held for review (card 3009). What was removed, moved or switched off on this server since the backup comes back held, paused or off (card 3004) |
| **Restore as a working copy** | A copy that must not reach outside: a development instance, a rehearsal, a look at older data | Report schedules and webhooks are switched off. The backup schedule is switched off and its destinations removed. The mail settings are removed. Every API key is left out (card 2995): no key of the backup works on the copy, and the result counts them; make new keys on the copy's Account page. Everything else is restored, users included |

A backup of production restored as it is into another instance is as able to email people, call webhooks and change the backup store as production is. That is what the working copy is for.

- **Which is preselected.** The working copy, when this instance's schedulers are off: under the development server, or with `SCHEDULERS=off`. Otherwise "as it is". Either can be chosen wherever the dialog opens.
- **It is part of the restore.** The switching off happens in the same transaction as the copy, so a restore that fails leaves everything as it was.
- **The result says what was done**, and stays on the page until it is dismissed: how many report schedules and webhooks were switched off, whether the backup schedule was on, and how many destinations and mail settings were removed. The webhooks count every one the backup had on, a webhook switched off in the backup for want of its secret included. A backup made since card 2169 has no mail password, so it removes one mail setting fewer than an older one did.
- **Its webhooks with no secret are listed.** A working copy names no mail password and no destination to enter, since it removes them, but its "To enter again" line names each webhook that lost its secret in the backup and has none here: whoever switches one on in the copy sets its secret first. No start-up line names them afterwards: a working copy's webhooks are off on purpose.
- **Getting them back.** A report schedule is switched on again by its owner on the **Reports** page, one at a time (only its owner sees it there), and is sent at its next time, not at once (card 2159). A webhook is switched on with `PUT /api/v1/webhooks/{id}` and `{ "active": true }`, by the board's owner or an administrator (card 2154: an editor is refused `403`), with their API key; no page switches a webhook on. Mail settings and backup destinations were removed and have to be entered again, or restored by importing the backup once more as it is.
- **Reload before looking.** The Scheduled Backups and mail panels are read when the page opens. After an import, use "Dismiss and reload", and do not save from a panel that was open before it.
- **By request.** `POST /api/admin/import` takes the same choice as `?outside=keep` or `?outside=off`, with the same default when it is not given. Any other value is refused and nothing is changed.

Whichever is chosen:

- Once it is committed, the restored mail settings, App URL and backup destinations of anyone recorded as their setter who is not an administrator in the restored data, or whose account it does not have, are removed (card 2411), as every start removes them (card 2406): a backup taken after an administrator was made a user from the host, and before the restart that would have removed what they set, brought those back in force until the next start. The system log has the same `WARN` lines under `SMTP` and `BACKUP`, ending `(found after a restore)`, and the result on the page adds a line, "Removed after the restore, because whoever set them is not an administrator in the restored data, or has no account in it: …", naming each: the mail settings (so no mail is sent, invites and password resets included, until an administrator sets the mail up again), the App URL, the destinations. A mail password or destination removed so is not listed as kept or to enter again. A working copy has already removed the mail settings and destinations, so only its App URL can go. To see beforehand whom a backup would lose settings for, run the read-only query of the deployment guide's check 461 against the backup file: copy it into the container (`docker cp <backup>.db <container>:/tmp/check.db`, then `docker exec -u root <container> chown node /tmp/check.db`, since `docker cp` writes it as root and the server's user since card 2776 is `node`) and use `'/tmp/check.db'` where that command has `process.env.DB_PATH || 'dumpfire.db'`; rows whose `role` is `user`, or `null` with a `user_id`, are the ones that go. Remove the copy afterwards (`docker exec -u root <container> rm /tmp/check.db`). It is done after the restore, on its own: should it fail, the restore stands, nothing of it is removed, one `[WARN] [IMPORT] The settings of setters who are not administrators in the restored data could not be checked: …` line says so, and the next start tries again.
- It replaces rows, not structure. The rows of every table in the running database are replaced with the backup's rows. The tables and columns stay as the running version's migrations made them: a column or table that exists only in the backup is not brought across, and a table the backup does not have is left empty.
- It keeps the running database's own record of applied migrations. That record describes the structure of the running database, which a restore does not change.
- It fills in what an older backup lacks: a first-completion date for cards that were complete, and the contents of `work_dependencies` when the backup was taken before that table existed. Every task request is given a new access token in any case (see above).
- It takes out of its milestone every card that sits in a milestone of another board (card 2179), which a backup taken before migration 0057 can hold: a milestone of a board holds only that board's cards, or the board's readers would read the other cards' titles in it. The card itself is untouched, and a goal across boards keeps its cards. The restore's "Filled in" line on the Logs page then ends `<n> card(s) taken out of a milestone of another board (card 12 left milestone 3, …)`, naming the first 50 cards by id with the milestone each left, and `; and <n> more` after them; with none, the line reads as it did. Once a card is out nothing records which goal held it, so if you want every card and goal by name, run check 0o's query 3 of the [deployment guide](deployment.md#before-the-deploy-checks-0o-to-0q) against the backup file, read-only, before importing it. The same repair runs at every start, where it finds nothing unless an older image was used meanwhile.
- It takes out of its grouping every board filed under a dashboard grouping that no longer exists (card 2293), which a backup taken before that card can hold: deleting a grouping used to leave its boards holding its id. Each such board was already shown as uncategorised. The restore's "Filled in" line then ends `<n> board(s) taken out of a grouping that no longer exists (board 2 (grouping 9), …)`, naming the first 50 boards by id with the grouping id each named, and `; and <n> more` after them; with none, the line reads as it did. The same repair runs at every start, where it finds nothing unless an older image was used meanwhile.
- It keeps the commitment point (milestone 22, migration 0060).
